Quantum speed-ups for solving semidefinite relaxations of polynomial optimization
Daniel Stilck França, Ngoc Hoang Anh Mai

Abstract
We study quantum algorithms for approximating Lasserre’s hierarchy values for polynomial optimization. Let f , g 1 , … , g m f,g_{1},\ldots,g_{m} be real polynomials in n n variables and f ⋆ f^{\star} the infimum of f f over the semialgebraic set S ⁡ ( g ) = { x : g i ​ ( x ) ≥ 0 } S(g)=\{x:g_{i}(x)\geq 0\} . Let λ k \lambda_{k} be the value of the order- k k Lasserre relaxation. Assume either (i) f ⋆ = λ k f^{\star}=\lambda_{k} and the optimum is attained in the ℓ 1 \ell_{1} -ball of radius 1 / 2 1/2 , or (ii) S ⁡ ( g ) S(g) lies in the simplex { x ≥ 0 : ∑ j x j ≤ 1 / 2 } \{x\geq 0:\sum_{j}x_{j}\leq 1/2\} , and the constraints define this simplex. After an appropriate coefficient rescaling, we give a quantum algorithm based on matrix multiplicative weights that approximates λ k \lambda_{k} to accuracy ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 with runtime, for fixed k k , O ⁡ ( n k ​ ε − 4 + n k / 2 ​ ε − 5 ) , O ⁡ ( s g ​ [ n k ​ ε − 4 + ( n k + ∑ i = 1 m n k − d i ) 1 / 2 ​ ε − 5 ] ) , O(n^{k}\varepsilon^{-4}+n^{k/2}\varepsilon^{-5}),\qquad O\!\left(s_{g}\!\left[n^{k}\varepsilon^{-4}+\!\left(n^{k}+\!\sum_{i=1}^{m}n^{k-d_{i}}\right)^{1/2}\!\varepsilon^{-5}\right]\right), where s g s_{g} bounds the sparsity of the coefficient-matching matrices associated with the constraints. Classical matrix multiplicative-weights methods scale as O ⁡ ( n 3 ​ k ​ poly ​ ( 1 / ε ) ) O(n^{3k}\mathrm{poly}(1/\varepsilon)) even in the unconstrained case. As an example, we obtain an O ⁡ ( n ​ ε − 4 + n ​ ε − 5 ) O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5}) quantum algorithm for portfolio optimization, improving over the classical O ⁡ ( n ω + 1 ​ log ⁡ ( 1 / ε ) ) O(n^{\omega+1}\log(1/\varepsilon)) bound with ω ≈ 2.373 \omega\approx 2.373 . Our approach builds on and sharpens the analysis of Apeldoorn and Gilyén for the SDPs arising in polynomial optimization. We also show how to implement the required block encodings without QRAM. Under the stated assumptions, our method achieves a super-quadratic speedup in the problem dimension for computing Lasserre relaxations.

Keywords:
polynomial optimization, moment, sum of squares, Lasserre’s hierarchy, semidefinite programming, matrix multiplicative weights, quantum computing, quantum optimization, quantum algorithm, block encoding, portfolio optimization

1
Introduction

Semidefinite programming (SDP) plays a central role in modern optimization, appearing in diverse areas such as combinatorial optimization
[
17
]
, quantum information theory
[
7
]
, and polynomial optimization
[
29
,
35
]
. Although SDPs are convex, they often serve as tractable relaxations of challenging non-convex problems, making them indispensable tools for both theoretical analysis and practical computation.

Recent developments in quantum algorithms have shown that SDPs can, in principle, be solved with polynomial speedups over the best-known classical methods
[
7
,
43
,
4
,
44
,
50
]
. Many of these quantum algorithms, including those based on the multiplicative weights update method
[
3
,
2
,
27
]
, achieve this advantage under specific conditions on the input instances.
In particular, for quantum algorithms rooted in the matrix multiplicative weights (MMW) framework, it is crucial that the SDPs are presented in standard form, are properly normalized and we have an upper bound on the trace of a solution.
This requires bounding the operator norm of all constraint matrices, as well as establishing a known bound on the trace of an optimal solution. Furthermore, these quantum solvers often exhibit a suboptimal dependence on the accuracy parameter, so demonstrating that low-accuracy solutions are sufficient for a given application is essential to realizing genuine quantum advantages. Finally, most quantum speedups also need that the matrices defining the constraints and the objective function are highly sparse and we can easily obtain block encodings for them. Analysing these aspects of the quantum solvers is crucial, as some works indicate that such details can cause quantum algorithms for SDPs to only offer advantages at prohibitively large scales
[
24
]
.

In this work, we investigate these normalization, stability and structural requirements for a broad family of SDPs that arise as sums-of-squares (SOS)
[
29
]
relaxations from both unconstrained and inequality constrained polynomial optimization problems (POPs). Our contributions are twofold. First, we show how to transform these SDPs into sparse standard form with proper normalization, deriving bounds on relevant matrix quantities along the way. Second, we provide stability estimates for both constrained and unconstrained variants of these problems.
As such, we identify regimes for which the quantum algorithms offer a super-quadratic speedup compared to classical algorithms, but at the cost of a polynomial scaling in a precision parameter
\epsilon^{-1}
.
In particular, we show how to obtain stability results for the POPs in terms of
\ell_{1}
norm estimates of solutions assuming the hierarchy converged, making optimization problems over the simplex natural candidates for our techniques.
As an application, we show how to obtain super-quadratic speedups to solve portfolio optimization problems, which is an optimization problem over the simplex.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
reviews the necessary background on semidefinite programming, polynomial optimization, and the framework for achieving quantum speedups, along with a summary of our main contributions. In Section
3
, we introduce the notation, definitions, and preliminary results used throughout the paper, including discussions on strong duality, the exactness of SDP relaxations for POPs, the correctness of Hamiltonian updates, and the accuracy of approximate values obtained via their combination with binary search. Section
4
describes how quantum SDP solvers can be employed to solve SOS relaxations for specific classes of POPs, and demonstrates their application to the portfolio optimization problem. Finally, Section
5
concludes with a discussion of potential extensions and open research directions.

2
Background and Summary of results

2.1
Semidefinite programming

Consider the following standard form of a semidefinite program (SDP):

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{X,z}&\tr(CX)\\
\text{s.t.}&X=\diag(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})\in\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{S}^{N_{j}}_{+}\,,\,z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t}\,,\\
&\tr(A_{i}X)+q_{i}^{\top}z=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\end{array}

(1)

where
C,A_{i}\in\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{S}^{N_{j}}
,
q_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{t}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
are given data.
Here,
\mathbb{S}^{K}
(resp.
\mathbb{S}^{K}_{+}
) denotes the space of real symmetric matrices (resp. cone of positive semidefinite matrices) of size
K
, and
N=\max\{N_{1},\dots,N_{r}\}
is the size of the largest block.

Although problem (
1
) is convex, it is crucial for tackling non-convex problems commonly arising in practice in various areas, such as combinatorial optimization
[
15
,
42
]
, quantum information theory
[
13
,
6
]
, and polynomial optimization
[
42
,
21
,
39
,
31
]
, among others. Consequently, the development of efficient algorithms for solving semidefinite programs (SDPs) is of significant importance. Existing methods for solving SDPs include interior-point methods
[
45
,
25
,
9
]
, Newton-type/IPM variants
[
25
]
, bundle methods
[
19
,
18
]
, and augmented Lagrangian frameworks
[
51
]
.
From a complexity and practicality standpoint, many classical SDP solvers suffer from high polynomial dependence on the matrix dimension and accuracy, and often require substantial memory due to storing dense iterates/factorizations; first-order or low-rank variants mitigate memory to some extent but typically weaken guarantees. There are also quantum proposals, based either on multiplicative weights
[
3
,
7
,
43
,
37
,
16
]
or on quantum interior-point techniques
[
4
]
, which promise polynomial speedups under suitable normalization and input-access assumptions but inherit sensitivity to accuracy/conditioning and the need for careful instance scaling.

This paper focuses on the complexity and accuracy of SDP-solving algorithms based on matrix multiplicative weights solvers (MMW), both classical and quantum, in the context of polynomial optimization problems.

When
r=1
and
t=0
, the standard SDP (
1
) simplifies to:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(CX)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(2)

Its dual is given by:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau^{\star}=\sup\limits_{\xi}&b^{\top}\xi\\
\text{s.t.}&\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\,,\,C-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}A_{i}\succeq 0\,.\end{array}

(3)

The interior-point method introduced by Helmberg et al.
[
20
]
solves (
2
)-(
3
) using second-order information to approximate primal-dual solutions.
According to Jiang et al.
[
25
]
, its time complexity on a classical computer is:

O\left(N^{1/2}\left(MN^{2}+M^{\omega}+N^{\omega}\right)\log\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\right)\,,

(4)

where
\omega\in[2,2.38]
.
Augustino et al.
[
4
]
show that the complexity of solving (
2
) using quantum interior-point method is:

O\left(N^{5.5}\varepsilon^{-1}\ \text{polylog}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\right)\,.

(5)

If
M=O(N^{2})
, the complexity of quantum interior-point method reduces to:

O\left(N^{3.5}\varepsilon^{-1}\ \text{polylog}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\right)

(6)

As shown in Lemma
65
, the interior-point methods above provide approximate values for the optimal values
\lambda^{\star}
and
\tau^{\star}
of the semidefinite programs (
1
)-(
3
), with an accuracy of

\varepsilon N\,.

(7)

Arora and Kale
[
3
]
proposed the matrix multiplication weight (MMW) update algorithm, which is paired with binary search to solve SDP (
2
) in a running time of

O((MsN+N^{\omega})\varepsilon^{-2})\,,

(8)

where
\omega\approx 2.373
is the exponent of matrix multiplication and
s
represents the maximum number of nonzero entries per row in
A_{i}
s and
C
(see Lemma
7
).
Unlike the interior-point method, which determines a search direction by solving a linear system of equations to update the iterates, variations of MMW like the Hamiltonian updates algorithm
[
16
]
adjusts the iterates by verifying infeasibility across affine constraints relative to the parameter
\varepsilon
, without relying on solving linear systems.

Quantum algorithms for SDPs based on MMW were introduced by Brandão and Svore
[
7
]
, with further optimizations developed in
[
43
,
16
]
. The key to their super-quadratic speedup lies in two ingredients:
(i)
quantum Gibbs sampling
subroutines, which allow efficient preparation of quantum states proportional to
\exp(-H)
for relevant matrix inputs
H
, thereby reducing the dependence on the matrix dimension from
N
to
\sqrt{N}
in the runtime; and
(ii)
gentle measurement
techniques, which enable constraint verification while disturbing the quantum state only minimally, leading to a
\sqrt{M}
scaling in the number of constraints.
Both ingredients rely on the ability to implement
block encodings
of the relevant matrices
A_{i}
and
C
, which can be nontrivial to implement in general
[
8
]
. Thus, one of our contributions is to construct efficient block encodings for the relevant matrices for our SDPs.

The running time of the quantum Hamiltonian updates algorithm for solving the SDP (
2
) is now:

O\left(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4}\right),

(9)

where
s
represents the maximum number of nonzero entries per row in
A_{i}
s and
C
.

As shown in the work of Apeldoorn and Gilyén
[
43
]
, their complexities (
9
) requires a constant upper bound on the trace of the optimal solution
X^{\star}
to the primal SDP (
2
), which can be replaced by a constant trace condition for
X^{\star}
by embedding the input matrices into larger ones).

Although the polynomial dependence on
\varepsilon^{-1}
makes Hamiltonian updates exponentially slower than classical interior-point methods in terms of precision, the complexity of Hamiltonian updates is of a lower order concerning problem size—specifically, the number of affine constraints
M
, the sparsity
s
and the size of the variable matrix
N
—compared to classical interior-point solvers, albeit assuming access to block encodings.

In Lemma
14
, we prove that under strong duality for the problems (
2
)-(
3
), the accuracy of the approximation for the optimal value
\lambda^{\star}
of the primal SDP (
2
), returned by Hamiltonian updates, is

\varepsilon(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\,,

(10)

where
\xi^{\star}
is an optimal solution to the dual SDP (
3
). In Table
1
we summarize the scaling of various SDP solvers.

Table 1:
Summary of classical and quantum algorithms based on Interior-point method and Hamiltonian updates for solving semidefinite programs (SDPs) (
2
)-(
3
) with matrix variable of size
N
and
M
affine equality constraints. Here
X^{\star}
is some optimal solution to SDP (
2
);
\xi^{\star}
is some optimal solution to SDP (
3
); and
s
is the largest number of nonzero entries on each row of
C,A_{i}
.

]
)

,

(Jiang et al.
[
25
]
)

\varepsilon N

(Lemma
65
)

Quantum

O\left(N^{5.5}\varepsilon^{-1}\ \text{polylog}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\right)

(Augustino et al.
[
4
]
)

O\left(N^{3.5}\varepsilon^{-1}\ \text{polylog}\left(\varepsilon^{-1}\right)\right)

(Augustino et al.
[
4
]
)

(Matrix multiplicative weight update)

(Arora and Kale
[
3
]
)

,

-I_{N}\preceq C\preceq I_{N}
,

-I_{N}\preceq A_{i}\preceq I_{N}

(Lemma
7
)

(Lemma
14
)

Quantum

O\left(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4}\right)

(Apeldoorn and Gilyén
[
43
]
)

In this paper, we apply the Hamiltonian update algorithm to estimate the optimal values of the semidefinite relaxations for the polynomial optimization problem presented in the next section. The key idea behind our main results is that we convert the semidefinite relaxations for the polynomial optimization problem to standard form (
2
) that satisfy the scaling condition on the input data as well as the constant trace condition for
X^{\star}
, then we compute the sparsity parameter
s
for the complexity and compute upper bound for the quantity

\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}
for the accuracy, identifying certain regimes that admit quantum speedups.

2.2
Polynomial optimization

Consider the general form of polynomial optimization problem (POPs) involving
n
variables,
m
inequality constraints, and
l
equality constraints:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f^{\star}=\min\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}&f(x)\\
\text{s.t.}&g_{i}(x)\geq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\,,\\
&h_{j}(x)=0\,,\,j=1,\dots,l\,,\\
\end{array}

(11)

where
f,g_{i}
, and
h_{j}
are given polynomials with real coefficients and degrees at most
2d,2d_{i}
, and
2w_{j}
, respectively. We also set
d_{0}=0
, which is the degree of an external constraint
g_{0}=1
used later.

POPs of the form (
11
) have numerous significant practical applications, including optimal power flow
[
48
,
38
]
, optimal control
[
22
]
, machine learning
[
32
]
, and data analysis
[
33
]
. However, solving (
11
) is highly challenging: it is typically non-convex and, in general, NP-hard
[
34
]
.

The constraint set for the problem in (
11
), denoted by
S(g,h)
, is a semialgebraic set defined as:

S(g,h)=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,g_{i}(x)\geq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\,,\,h_{j}(x)=0\,,\,j=1,\dots,l\}\,,

(12)

where
g=(g_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
and
h=(h_{j})_{j=1}^{l}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
.
Here,
{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{t}={\mathbb{R}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]_{d}
denotes the ring of polynomials in
x
of degree at most
t
. Note that
x
is a vector of
n
scalar variables, i.e.,

x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,.

(13)

An important tool to solve optimization problems like (
11
) are sums-of-squares polynomials:

Definition 1

(Sum–of–squares (SOS) cone)
.

Fix integers
n\geq 1
(number of variables) and
d\geq 0
(half-degree).
The
SOS cone of degree
2d
in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
is

\Sigma[x]_{d}\;:=\;\Bigl\{p\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}\;\Bigm|\;\exists\;q_{1},\dots,q_{s}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{d}\text{ such that }p(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{s}q_{i}(x)^{2}.\Bigr\},

It is well-known
[
29
]
that the sum-of-squares relaxation:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,\sigma_{i},\psi_{j}}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma[x]_{k-d_{i}}\,,\,\psi_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2(k-w_{j})}\,,\\
&f-\lambda=\sigma_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}g_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\psi_{j}h_{j}\,.\end{array}

(14)

produces a sequence of non-decreasing lower bounds
(\lambda_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
for the optimal value
f^{\star}
.

Before we further explore the structure of the problem (
14
), we introduce some further standard definitions for moment/sum-of-squares relaxations:

Definition 2

(Multi-index set)
.

For non-negative integers
k,n
set

{\mathbb{N}}_{2k}^{\,n}\;:=\;\bigl\{\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\;:\;|\alpha|:=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}\leq 2k\bigr\}.

It indexes all monomials
x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}

of total degree at most
2k
.

Definition 3

(Truncated moment sequence)
.

A vector
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{2k}^{\,n}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}

is called a
truncated moment sequence of order
2k
with respect to a positive measure
\mu
if

y_{\alpha}=\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}x^{\alpha}\,d\mu(x)\,.

(15)

Definition 4

(Riesz linear functional)
.

Given a vector
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{2k}^{\,n}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,
the
Riesz functional

L_{y}:{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}},\qquad L_{y}\!\Bigl(\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\Bigr)=\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}\,y_{\alpha},

acts by replacing each monomial coefficient
x^{\alpha}
with
its corresponding moment
y_{\alpha}
.

Definition 5

(Moment matrix)
.

For an integer
k\geq 0
,
the
moment matrix of order
k
associated with
y
is

M_{k}(y):=\bigl(y_{\alpha+\beta}\bigr)_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}_{k}^{\,n}}.

Rows and columns are indexed by monomials of degree
\leq t
.

Definition 6

(Localizing matrix)
.

Let
p(x)=\sum_{\gamma}p_{\gamma}x^{\gamma}
be a polynomial of degree at most

2u
and fix
k\geq u
.
The
localizing matrix of order
k-u
associated with
p
and
y
is

M_{k-u}(p\,y):=\Bigl(\sum_{\gamma}p_{\gamma}\,y_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma}\Bigr)_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}_{k-u}^{\,n}}.

The sum-of-squares relaxation (
14
) can be equivalently formulated as the following semidefinite program:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G_{i},\eta_{j}}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,i=0,\dots,m\,,\,\eta_{j}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,j=1,\dots,l\,,\\
&f-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}G_{0}v_{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}h_{j}\eta_{j}^{\top}v_{2(k-w_{j})}\,,\end{array}

(16)

where
v_{t}
is the vector of monomials up to degree
t
.

The dual of (
16
), known as moment relaxations, are formulated as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau_{k}=\min\limits_{y}&L_{y}(f)\\
\text{s.t.}&y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\,,\\
&M_{k}(y)\succeq 0\,,\,y_{0}=1\,,\\
&M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y)\succeq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\,,\\
&M_{k-w_{j}}(h_{j}y)=0\,,\,j=1,\dots,l\,,\end{array}

(17)

where
M_{t}(y)
is the moment matrix of order
k
associated with
y
,
M_{t-u}(py)
is the localizing matrix of order
t-u
associated with
y
and a polynomial
p
of degree at most
2u
, and
L_{y}
is the Riesz linear functional on
{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2t}
.

The formulations (
16
)-(
17
) are primal-dual semidefinite programs originally introduced by Lasserre in
[
29
]
.
When
g_{1}
represents a ball constraint, i.e.,
g_{1}=R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
for some
R>0
, Lasserre demonstrated that the sequence
(\lambda_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
converges monotonically to
f^{\star}
from below
1
1

1

The convergence rate of the sequence
(\lambda_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
to
f^{\star}
is discussed in detail in
[
5
,
36
]
. In this work, however, our focus is on analyzing the computational complexity involved in solving each semidefinite relaxation.

.
In this case, Josz and Henrion showed in
[
26
]
that
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
for all
k\geq\max\{d,d_{1},\dots,d_{m},w_{1},\dots,w_{l}\}
.

Thus, the optimal values of (
16
)-(
17
),
\lambda_{k}
and
\tau_{k}
, serve as lower approximations for the optimal value
f^{\star}
of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
).
These values enable us to determine whether a local optimal solution
\bar{x}\in S(g,h)
for problem (
16
), obtained using classical optimization methods, is a global minimum or not. This is achieved by evaluating the gap
f(\bar{x})-\lambda_{k}
.
Since
\lambda_{k}\leq f^{\star}\leq f(\bar{x})
, a zero gap
f(\bar{x})-\lambda_{k}=0
implies that
f(\bar{x})=f^{\star}
, confirming global optimality. Furthermore, if the moment matrix satisfies certain rank constraints and the hierarchy has converged, it is possible to extract the solutions from it
[
23
]
.

However, solving (
16
)-(
17
) to compute
\lambda_{k}
and
\tau_{k}
becomes increasingly challenging because their size grows rapidly as the relaxation order
k
increases.
To illustrate this, we reformulate problems (
16
) and (
17
) in the standard form of a semidefinite program (
1
).

For the sum-of-squares relaxation (
1
), the corresponding semidefinite program (
1
) has:

M=\binom{n+2k}{n}
affine equality constraints,

t=\sum_{j=1}^{l}\binom{n+2(k-w_{j})}{n}=O\left(l\binom{n+2k}{n}\right)

(18)

scalar variables, and
r=m+1
positive semidefinite matrices, with the largest size
N=\binom{n+k}{n}
.

For the moment relaxation (
17
), the corresponding semidefinite program (
1
) has:

\begin{array}[]{rl}M=&1+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\binom{n+2(k-w_{j})}{n}+\frac{1}{2}\binom{n+k}{n}[\binom{n+k}{n}+1]-\binom{n+2k}{n}\\[5.0pt]
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}[\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}+1]\\[5.0pt]
=&O\left(l\binom{n+2k}{n}+m\binom{n+k}{n}^{2}\right)\end{array}

(19)

affine equality constraints,
t=0
scalar variables and
r=m+1
positive semidefinite matrices, with the largest size
N=\binom{n+k}{n}
.

These complexities highlight the exponential growth in size with respect to number of variables
n
of the polynomial optimization (
11
) and the relaxation order
k
of the semidefinite relaxations (
2
)-(
3
), making these relaxations computationally intensive to solve.

Therefore, we seek a method for solving the semidefinite relaxations (
2
)-(
3
) that maintains low computational complexity relative to the problem size and does not demand high precision. The method employed below fulfills these expectations.

2.3
Block encodings

To efficiently apply the Matrix Multiplicative Weights method/Hamiltonian updates (Algorithms
1
and
2
) for solving the SDP relaxation of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
), we adopt the
block-encoding
framework
[
14
]
.
This formalism provides an efficient mechanism for accessing large, structured matrices, thereby enabling significant algorithmic speed-ups in large-scale SDP solvers.

A block encoding represents a matrix
A
as a normalized sub-block of a unitary operator
U
such that

U=\begin{pmatrix}A/\alpha&*\\
*&*\end{pmatrix},

where
\alpha>0
is a known normalization factor.

In Appendix
F
, we describe how to construct block encodings for the matrices
A_{i}
obtained when converting the SOS relaxation (
16
) (with
l=0
) into the standard SDP form (
2
).
This construction only requires oracle access to the coefficients of the polynomials
f
and
g_{i}
. The resulting block encodings can be generated using
O(n\log k)
quantum gates, ensuring that this step does not constitute a computational bottleneck for the overall method.

2.4
Our contribution

In this paper, we develop quantum algorithms for solving the semidefinite relaxations (
16
)-(
17
) of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
). Our approach utilizes binary search and Hamiltonian updates
[
16
]
.

Our main contributions are:

•

We show that, after appropriate rescaling of the objective and constraints, the SOS relaxations of certain POPs admit a bounded-trace feasible region. This bound is key for analysing the scaling of the SDPs.

•

We identify settings—both unconstrained and inequality-constrained POPs—where the SDP data is sparse (each row of
C
and
A_{i}
has few nonzero entries), enabling the Hamiltonian updates framework to offer runtime improvements over classical methods.

•

For each setting, we prove runtime bounds and accuracy guarantees for our quantum algorithms based on various structural assumptions on the POP, such as the solution being in a certain
\ell_{1}
ball or the hierarchy converging after a certain number of levels.

Leveraging these contributions we obtain the following results:

•

Case 1: Unconstrained polynomial optimization
(see Section
4.1
).

Consider the unconstrained case of problem (
11
), where
m=l=0
.

If the optimal value of the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
) of some order
k
matches the optimal value of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
), i.e.,
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
, and
f^{\star}
is attained at a point within an
\ell_{1}
-ball of radius
r\in(0,1)
centered at the origin, then our quantum Algorithm
3
for solving the (
16
) has a runtime of

O\left(\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\binom{n+k}{n}^{1/2}\ \right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

(20)

to compute an approximate value of
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-r}
(see Theorem
1
).

To obtain the complexity (
20
), we transform the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
) to standard semidefinite program (
2
) and apply Apeldoorn–Gilyén’s result
[
43
]
.
Note that each row of the input matrices
C
and
A_{i}
in program (
2
) has at most one nonzero entry.
In addition, the exactness assumption
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
allows us to bound the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the dual solution of program (
2
) via the bound
r
on the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the optimal solution for the polynomial optimization problem (
11
).

•

Case 2: Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization problem
(see Sections
4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
).

Now consider the inequality-constrained case of problem (
11
), where
l=0
.

(i) If
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
and
f^{\star}
is attained at a point within an
\ell_{1}
-ball of radius
r\in(0,1)
centered at the origin, with a coefficient rescaling of the
g_{i}
s, our quantum Algorithm
4
for solving sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
) requires

O\left(s_{g}\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)\,,

(21)

to provide an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-r}
, where
s_{g}
is the maximum number of nonzero entries per row of the
coefficient-matching matrices
(defined as in (
117
)) associated with
g_{i}
s (see Theorem
2
).
Note that when the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
) is transformed to standard semidefinite program (
2
), each row of
C
and
A_{i}
s has at most
s_{g}
nonzero entries and
s_{g}
is not larger than the maximum number of nonzero coefficients of each
g_{i}
.

Consequently, if
f^{\star}
is attained at a point within an
\ell_{1}
-ball of radius
r\in(0,1)
centered at the origin, and the coefficients of
g_{i}
s are rescaled appropriately, while the second-order sufficient optimality conditions hold for problem (
11
) at every optimal solution, then for sufficiently large
k
, Algorithm
4
yields an approximate value for
f^{\star}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-r}
(see Corollary
1
).

Instead of using the exactness assumption
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
, we can rely on the inequality constraints (ball and simplex) in the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) to bound the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the dual solution of the relaxation program (
2
).

(ii) Concretely, if the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) includes a ball constraint of the form
c(R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2})\geq 0
with
R\in(0,1)
and
c>0
, then after rescaling the coefficients of the
g_{i}
constraints, Algorithm
4
produces an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-R}\binom{n+k}{n}
(see Theorem
3
).

(iii) If the constraint set
S(g,h)
of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) is contained within the simplex
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\,:\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\leq r\}
for some
r\in(0,1)
, meaning that the terms
x^{\alpha}
, for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, and polynomials
c_{j}[r^{j}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j}]
with
c_{j}>0
, for
j=1,2
, are included among
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
, then with coefficient rescaling of the
g_{i}
s, our Algorithm
4
provides an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-r}
(see Theorem
4
).

(iv) To avoid the exponential number of inequality constraints in
S(g,h)
in the last result, we define a new semidefinite program (
124
) with optimal value
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
by relaxing equality affine constraints in sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
).
When the constraint set
S(g,h)
is contained within the nonnegative orthant
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
, it holds that
\lambda_{k}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{k}\leq f^{\star}
.
If
S(g,h)
is contained within the simplex
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\,:\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\leq r\}
for some
r\in(0,1)
and polynomials
c_{j}[r^{j}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j}]
with
c_{j}>0
, for
j=1,2
, are included among
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
, our quantum Algorithm
5
provides an approximate value for
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
with an accuracy of
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-r}
and runtimes (
21
) (see Theorem
6
).

(v) By using our Algorithm
5
, the optimal value of the portfolio optimization problem can be approximated to within
\varepsilon
in time
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
on a quantum computer (see Theorem
7
).

In Table
2
we summarize the results for the cases considered above.
