In Appendix
B
, we discuss additional quantum complexities of SDP relaxations (both SOS and moment relaxations) for polynomial optimization problems (POPs) in case where the Slater’s conditions hold for these SDP relaxations. This appendix is divided into two parts:
In Appendix
B.1
, we present the computational complexities and accuracies of our quantum algorithms for solving SOS relaxations of POPs in more general settings where the exactness assumption
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
and the structural constraints discussed above do not hold. In Appendix
B.2
, we further apply our approach to moment relaxations of POPs over the unit ball and the unit sphere. However, the results in these more general cases are less conclusive compared to those reported in the main body of the paper.

2.5
Comparison with other methods

If we employ interior-point methods
[
20
,
25
,
4
]
to solve the sum-of-squares relaxation (
14
) of problem (
11
) with
m=l=0
in Case 1, the time complexity on a classical computer is

O\!\left(\binom{n+k}{n}^{1/2}\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}\binom{n+k}{n}^{2}+\binom{n+2k}{n}^{\omega}+\binom{n+k}{n}^{\omega}\right]\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right),

(22)

where
\omega\in[2,2.38]
.
On a quantum computer, the corresponding complexity becomes

O\!\left(\binom{n+k}{n}^{3.5}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{polylog}\!\left(\binom{n+k}{n},\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right),

(23)

to compute an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with accuracy

\varepsilon\,\binom{n+k}{n}.

(24)

Compared with these interior-point methods, our quantum algorithm (see (
20
)) exhibits a higher dependence on the precision parameter
\varepsilon
, scaling polynomially as
O((1/\varepsilon)^{5})
, whereas classical interior-point methods depend only logarithmically on
1/\varepsilon
and the quantum interior-point method scales as
O((1/\varepsilon)\log(1/\varepsilon))
.
However, our approach achieves substantially smaller exponents in terms of
\binom{n+k}{n}
and
\binom{n+2k}{n}
—more than four times lower than those in the interior-point setting—yielding superior asymptotic performance for large-scale polynomial optimization problems.
While the dependence on
\varepsilon
is worse, this trade-off is advantageous in high-dimensional regimes where problem size dominates the numerical precision requirements.

When
t=0
, the standard SDP (
1
) is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(CX)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}^{\bar{N}}_{+},\quad\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,M,\end{array}

(25)

where
\bar{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}N_{j}
,
C,A_{i}\in\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{S}^{N_{j}}_{+}
,
q_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{t}
, and
b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.
In this SDP, while the decision matrix
X
is dense, the matrices
A_{i}
and
C
exhibit block-diagonal structures.
The sum-of-squares relaxation (
14
) of problem (
11
) with
l=0
in Case 2 can thus be formulated as the semidefinite program (
25
) with
r=m+1
,
M=\binom{n+2k}{n}
, and

N_{i+1}=\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}.

(26)

Using an interior-point method to solve this SDP, the classical computational complexity is

\footnotesize O\!\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\!\left[\!\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{\!2}\!\binom{n+2k}{n}+\binom{n+2k}{n}^{\omega}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{\!\omega}\!\right]\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\!\right),

(27)

and on a quantum computer it is

O\!\left(\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{3.5}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\mathrm{polylog}\!\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right),

(28)

to approximate
\lambda_{k}
with accuracy

\varepsilon\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}.

(29)

Compared with these interior-point complexities, our quantum MMW-based algorithm (
21
) achieves a lower exponent in the parameters
\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}
(
\leq(m+1)\binom{n+k}{n}
), and
\binom{n+2k}{n}
(noting that
\binom{n+2k}{n}=O(\binom{n+k}{n}^{2})
), while exhibiting a higher dependence on
1/\varepsilon
.
Furthermore, if
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
and
f^{\star}
is attained at a point within an
\ell_{1}
-ball of radius
\frac{1}{2}
centered at the origin, or if the feasible set
S(g,h)
of problem (
11
) lies within the simplex
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}:\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\leq 1/2\}
, then under suitable coefficient rescaling of
f
and
g_{i}
, our quantum algorithm attains improved accuracy in
\varepsilon
.

For example, our quantum algorithm can approximate the optimal value of a portfolio optimization problem (with
k=d_{1}=\dots=d_{m}=1
,
s_{g}=1
and
m=O(n)
) to accuracy
\varepsilon
in computational time
O(n^{4}\operatorname{poly}(\varepsilon^{-1}))
on a classical computer and
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
on a quantum computer (see Theorem
7
).
For comparison, classical and quantum interior-point methods require
O(n^{3.5}\log(1/\varepsilon))
and
O(n^{4.5}\varepsilon^{-1}\log(\varepsilon^{-1}))
, respectively, to achieve the same level of accuracy.
Hence, our approach attains a super-quadratic speedup in the dependence on
n
relative to interior-point methods in this example.

Memory comparison.

In terms of memory usage, classical interior-point methods require storing and factorizing dense matrices of size
\bar{N}\times\bar{N}
, resulting in a memory cost of
O(\bar{N}^{2})
, and typically
O((M+\bar{N})^{3})
when forming and solving the normal equations at each iteration.
In contrast, in our MMW-based approach, the matrices
C
and
A_{j}
are assumed to be
sparse
, with at most
s
nonzero entries per row.
This sparsity substantially reduces storage requirements: only the positions and values of the nonzero elements need to be stored or accessed through oracles.
On a classical computer, this yields a memory cost of
O(s\bar{N})
when exploiting the block-diagonal and sparse structures of
A_{j}
and
C
.
On a quantum computer, these sparse matrices are represented by block encodings constructed from sparse-access oracles, which require only
\mathrm{polylog}(\bar{N})
qubits to store and query.
Consequently, our method is markedly more memory-efficient than interior-point approaches on both classical and quantum platforms, especially for large-scale SDP relaxations where the data matrices are structured and sparse.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Notation and definitions

Let
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{p}}
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, denote the
l_{p}
-norm of a real vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Then, for any
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the following inequality holds:
\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}\leq\|x\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\sqrt{n}\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}
.

The trace of a real square matrix
A
, denoted by
\tr(A)
, is the sum of its diagonal elements.
A real symmetric matrix
A
is said to be positive semidefinite (denoted by
A\succeq 0
) if all of its eigenvalues are nonnegative, and positive definite (denoted by
A\succ 0
) if all of its eigenvalues are positive.
We write
A\succeq B
(respectively
A\succ B
) to mean that
A-B\succeq 0
(respectively
A-B\succ 0
).
Let
\|\cdot\|_{F}
and
\|\cdot\|_{*}
represent the Frobenius and nuclear norms, respectively, of a real matrix
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
, i.e.,

\|A\|_{F}=\sqrt{\tr(A^{\top}A)}
and
\|A\|_{*}=\tr(\sqrt{A^{\top}A})
.
It follows that:
\|A\|_{F}\leq\|A\|_{*}
.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{N}
be the Hilbert space of real symmetric matrices of size
N
, equipped with the trace inner product
\tr(A^{\top}B)
and the Frobenius norm
\|A\|_{F}=\sqrt{\tr(A^{2})}
, for given
A,B\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.
We denote by
\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
the cone of positive semidefinite matrix in
\mathbb{S}^{N}
.
Let
I_{N}
denote the identity matrix of size
N
.
Given symmetric matrices
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
, let
\diag(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
denote the block-diagonal matrix with blocks
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
.
For
X=(X_{ij})_{i,j\in[N]}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, we define
\text{vec}(X)=\left(X_{ij}\sqrt{2-\delta_{ij}}\right)_{i\leq j}
, where
\delta_{ij}
equals
1
if
i=j
and is
0
otherwise.
Then,
\trace(XY)=\text{vec}(X)^{\top}\text{vec}(Y)
, for
X,Y\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.
It implies
\|X\|_{F}=\|\text{vec}(X)\|_{\ell_{2}}
.
Let
\text{mat}(\cdot)
denote the inverse map of
\text{vec}(\cdot)
.
If
X=\diag(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})\in\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathbb{S}^{N_{j}}=\mathbb{S}^{N_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{S}^{N_{r}}
, define
\text{Vec}(X)=(\text{vec}(X_{1}),\dots,\text{vec}(X_{r}))\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L}
, with
L=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{r}N_{i}(N_{i}+1)
.
Let
\text{Mat}(\cdot)
denote the inverse map of
\text{Vec}(\cdot)
.

Let
A
be a real matrix of size
m\times n
.
The
\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q}
norm of
A
is defined as

\|A\|_{\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q}}=\sup\{\|Ax\|_{\ell_{q}}\,:\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,,\,\|x\|_{\ell_{p}}=1\}
,
where
p,q\in[1,\infty]
.
It follows that
\|Ax\|_{\ell_{q}}\leq\|A\|_{\ell_{p}\to\ell_{q}}\|x\|_{\ell_{p}}
.
In particular,
\|A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}
represents the maximum
\ell_{1}
-norm of a column of
A
and
\|A\|_{\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}}
is the largest singular value of
A
.
Let
\sigma_{\max}(\cdot)
and
\sigma_{\min}(\cdot)
denote the largest and smallest singular value of a real matrix, respectively.
Similarly, let

\lambda_{\max}(\cdot)
and
\lambda_{\min}(\cdot)
represent the largest and smallest eigenvalues of a real symmetric matrix, respectively.
Denote by
\det(\cdot)
the determinant of a square matrix.
The condition number of a real symmetric matrix
A
is defined by
\kappa(A)=\frac{\lambda_{\max}(A)}{\lambda_{\min}(A)}
.

Let
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
denote the ring of polynomials in vectors of variables
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}=\{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\,;\,|\alpha|\leq k\}
, where
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}
.
For
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
, we write
\alpha\leq\beta
(in lexicographical order) if either
|\alpha|<|\beta|
or
|\alpha|=|\beta|
and
\alpha_{i}>\beta_{i}
for the largest
i
for which
\alpha_{i}\neq\beta_{i}
.
Let
v_{k}
denote the vector of monomials of degree up to
k
, i.e.,
v_{k}=(x^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}
with
x^{\alpha}=x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots x_{n}^{\alpha_{n}}
.
A polynomial
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
can be written as
p=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}p_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}=\bar{p}^{\top}v_{k}
with
\bar{p}=(p_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The degree of polynomial
p
is defined as
\deg(p)=\max\{|\alpha|\,:\,p_{\alpha}\neq 0\}
.
Let
{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{k}
denote the linear space of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
of degree at most
k
, and let
\Sigma[x]_{k}
represent the cone of sums of squares of polynomials in
{\mathbb{R}}[x]
of degree at most
2k
.

For
p\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, let

\|p\|_{\ell_{q}}
represent the
\ell_{q}
-norm of the vector of coefficients of
p
.
A polynomial
p=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}p_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
has its support defined as
\supp(p)=\{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\,:\,p_{\alpha}\neq 0\}
.
For
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
with
|\alpha|=k
, the multinomial coefficient
\binom{k}{\alpha}
is defined as
\binom{k}{\alpha}=\frac{k!}{\alpha_{1}!\dots\alpha_{n}!}
.

For a given
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
, let
M_{k}(y)
denote the moment matrix of order
k
associated with
y
, i.e.,
M_{k}(y)=(y_{\alpha+\beta})_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}
.
For
p=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2u}}p_{\gamma}x^{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2u}
, let
M_{k-u}(py)
denote the localizing matrix of order
k-u
associated with
p
and
y
of order
k
, i.e.,
M_{k-u}(py)=(\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2u}}p_{\gamma}y_{\alpha+\beta+\gamma})_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-u}}
.
Let
L_{y}:{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the Riesz linear functional defined by
L_{y}(q)=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}q_{\alpha}y_{\alpha}
, for a given
q=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}q_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
.
Then, we have the following relations for real symmetric matrices
G
and
Q
:
L_{y}(v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k})=\tr(GM_{k}(y))

and
L_{y}(pv_{k-u}^{\top}Qv_{k-u})=\tr(QM_{k-u}(py))
.

Let
\mu
be a positive measure on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. For
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
, the quantity
y_{\alpha}=\int x^{\alpha}d\mu
is called the moment of order
\alpha
with respect to
\mu
.
The collection of moments
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}
is referred to as the moment sequence with respect to
\mu
.
If we restrict the moments to a finite set of multi-indices
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{d}
, the sequence
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{d}}
is called the truncated moment sequence of order
d
with respect to
\mu
.
For a truncated moment sequence
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
, we define
M_{k}(\mu)=M_{k}(y)
as the moment matrix of order
k
with
\mu
.
Given
p=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2u}}p_{\gamma}x^{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2u}
, we denote by
M_{k-u}(p\mu)=M_{k-u}(py)
the localizing matrix with
p
and
\mu
of order
k-u
.
Additionally, the Riesz linear functional
L_{y}
is defined by
L_{y}(q)=\int qd\mu
, for
q\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
.
For real symmetric matrices
P
and
Q
, the following relations hold:
\int v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}d\mu=\tr(GM_{k}(\mu))

and
\int pv_{k-u}^{\top}Qv_{k-u}d\mu=\tr(QM_{k-u}(p\mu))
.

3.2
Strong duality and exactness for semidefinite relaxations of polynomial optimization

Before applying quantum SDP solvers to sums-of-squares relaxations, it is important to understand when these relaxations are well-behaved from an optimization perspective. In particular, we will review conditions under which strong duality holds and when the SDP hierarchy achieves exactness—that is, the relaxation value equals the true optimum after finitely many levels. This subsection recalls standard optimality conditions, duality theorems, and finite-convergence criteria for polynomial optimization problems, which we will later use to justify our accuracy assumptions and runtime guarantees.
Given
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}_{+}
and

\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}^{l}
, the Lagrangian for problem (
11
) is defined as:

\mathcal{L}(x,\eta,\gamma)\,:=\,f(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}g_{i}(x)-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}h_{j}(x)\,.

(30)

For
x\in S(g,h)
, the active set of inequality constraints is given by:

J(x):=\{\,i\in\{1,\dots,m\}:g_{i}(x)=0\,\}\,.

(31)

We say that the second-order sufficient optimality conditions (see
[
41
, Chapter 2]
) hold for problem (
11
) at
x^{\star}\in S(g,h)
if the following criteria are satisfied:

1.

Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) conditions: There exist
\eta^{\star}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}_{+}
and
\gamma^{\star}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{l}
such that

\nabla_{x}\mathcal{L}(x^{\star},\eta^{\star},\gamma^{\star})=0
and
\eta^{\star}_{i}\,g_{i}(x^{\star})=0
for all
i=1,\dots,m
.

2.

Strict complementarity:
\eta_{i}^{\star}+g_{i}(x^{\star})>0
, for all
i=1,\dots,m
.

3.

Linear independence constraint qualification: the family

\Lambda=\{\nabla g_{i}(x^{\star})\}_{i\in J(x^{\star})}\cup\{\nabla h_{j}(x^{\star})\}_{j=1}^{l}

(32)

is linearly independent.

4.

Positive definiteness of the Hessian:
u^{\top}\nabla_{x}^{2}\mathcal{L}(x^{\star},\eta^{\star},\gamma^{\star})u>0
for all
u\in\text{span}(\Lambda)^{\perp}\backslash\{0\}
.

Lemma 1
.

(Josz-Henrion
[
26
]
)
Let
f^{\star}
be as in (
11
).
Assume
g_{1}=R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
for some
R>0
.
Then, zero duality gap between the primal (
16
) and dual (
17
) holds, i.e.,
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
and
\tau_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
k\geq k_{\min}
, where

k_{\min}=\max\{d,d_{1},\dots,d_{m},w_{1},\dots,w_{l}\}\,.

(33)

Moreover, SDP (
17
) has an optimal solution.

Josz and Henrion proved that the set of optimal solutions of (
17
) is compact, ensuring existence of an optimal solution.

Lemma 2
.

(Lasserre
[
29
, Theorem 3.4 (a)]
)
If
S(g,h)
has nonempty interior, then Slater’s condition holds for the dual (
17
) for
k\geq k_{\min}
, where
k_{\min}
is defined as in (
33
).
In this case,
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
,
\tau_{k}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and the primal problem (
16
) has an optimal solution.

The quadratic module
Q_{k}(g,h)
associated with
g=(g_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
and
h=(h_{j})_{j=1}^{l}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
is given by:

Q_{k}(g,h)=\{\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sigma_{i}g_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\psi_{j}h_{j}\,:\,\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma[x]_{k-d_{i}}\,,\,\psi_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2(k-w_{j})}\}

(34)

where
g_{0}=1
and
d_{0}=0
.
We say that
S(g,h)
is Archimedean if
R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}\in Q_{k}(g,h)
for some
R>0
.

The ideal
I(h)
associated with
h=(h_{j})_{j=1}^{l}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
is given by
I(h)=\{\sum_{j=1}^{l}\psi_{j}h_{j}\,:\,\psi_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]\}
.
We say that
I(h)
is real radical if for all
f\in I(h)
, there exist
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a sum-of-squares polynomial
\sigma
such that
-f^{2m}\in\sigma+I(h)
.

The following proposition provides a sufficient condition to ensure finite convergence of the sequence
(\lambda_{k})_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}
to
f^{\star}
.

Lemma 3
.

(Lasserre
[
34
, Theorem 7.5]
) Assume that
S(g,h)
is Archimedean, the ideal
I(h)
is real radical, and the second-order sufficient optimality conditions hold for problem (
11
) at every optimal solution.
Then,
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}=f^{\star}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and both primal and dual problems (
16
)-(
17
) admit optimal solutions.

It is worth noting that the real radical property is not generic, and verifying
I(h)
is real radical must be done on a case-by-case basis.
However, Nie
[
40
]
showed that generically,
\tau_{k}=f^{\star}
for sufficiently large
k
, meaning this equality holds on a Zariski open set in the space of coefficients of
g_{i},h_{j}
for fixed degrees.

The following lemma states that finite convergence occurs at the first order relaxation for convex quadratic programs.

Lemma 4

(Exactness for convex quadratic programs)
.

Let
f=x^{\top}Ax+q^{\top}x
with
A\succeq 0
,
g_{i}=a_{i}^{\top}x+b_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, and
h_{j}=c_{j}^{\top}x+w_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,l
.
Assume that problem (
11
) has an optimal solution
x^{\star}
.
Then
\lambda_{1}=f^{\star}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\lambda_{1}\leq f^{\star}
.
To prove the reverse inequality, we apply the KKT conditions for problem
f^{\star}=\min_{x\in S(g,h)}f(x)
at
x^{\star}
.
Then there exist
\eta^{\star}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}_{+}
and
\gamma^{\star}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{l}
such that

\eta_{i}^{\star}\,g_{i}(x^{\star})=0
for all
i=1,\dots,m
, and
\nabla f(x^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}^{\star}\nabla g_{i}(x^{\star})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}^{\star}\nabla h_{j}(x^{\star})
.
This follows that

\begin{array}[]{rl}&2Ax^{\star}+q=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}^{\star}a_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}^{\star}c_{j}\\
\Rightarrow&2(x-x^{\star})^{\top}Ax^{\star}+q^{\top}(x-x^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}^{\star}a_{i}^{\top}(x-x^{\star})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}^{\star}c_{j}^{\top}(x-x^{\star})\\
\Rightarrow&x^{\top}Ax+q^{\top}x-f^{\star}=(x-x^{\star})^{\top}A(x-x^{\star})+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}^{\star}(a_{i}^{\top}x+b_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}^{\star}(c_{j}^{\top}x+w_{j}).\end{array}

(35)

The last equality is due to
f^{\star}=x^{\star\top}Ax^{\star}+q^{\top}x^{\star}
,
0=\eta_{i}^{\star}\,g_{i}(x^{\star})=\eta^{\star}_{i}(a_{i}^{\top}x^{\star}+b_{i})
and
0=h_{j}(x^{\star})=c_{j}^{\top}x^{\star}+w_{j}
.
It implies that
f-f^{\star}=\sigma_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}^{\star}g_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{j}^{\star}h_{j}
, where
\sigma_{0}=(x-x^{\star})^{\top}A(x-x^{\star})
is a sum-of-squares polynomial of degree
2
(since
A\succeq 0
).
By definition of
\lambda_{k}
in (
14
),
f^{\star}\leq\lambda_{1}
.
Hence the equality holds.
∎

3.3
Hamiltonian updates

We are now going to briefly review the Hamiltonian updates framework to solve semidefinite programs of
[
16
]
.
Consider the semidefinite feasibility problem with trace-one constraint:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\text{Find}&X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}\\
\text{s.t.}&X\succeq 0\,,\,\tr(X)=1\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\end{array}

(36)

where
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for
i=1,\dots,M
.

We propose the following algorithm to solve problem (
36
) approximately.

Algorithm 1
.

Hamiltonian updates

•

Input:
\varepsilon>0
,
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i=1,\dots,M
.

•

Output:
\eta\in\{0,1\}
and
X_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Initialize
W^{(1)}=I_{N}
and set
T=\left\lceil\frac{16\log(N)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right\rceil
.

2.

For
t=1,\dots,T
:

(a)

Compute
P^{(t)}=W^{(t)}/\tr(W^{(t)})
.

(b)

Check feasibility:

i.

If there exists
j^{(t)}\in\{1,\dots,M\}
such that

\tr(A_{j^{(t)}}P^{(t)})>b_{j^{(t)}}+\varepsilon\,,

(37)

set
M^{(t)}=\frac{1}{2}(I_{N}-A_{j^{(t)}})
.

ii.

Otherwise, set
\eta=1
,
X_{\varepsilon}=P^{(t)}
and terminate.

(c)

Update
W^{(t+1)}=\exp(-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}M^{(\tau)})
.

3.

If the loop completes, set
\eta=0
and terminate.

Set the set
S_{\varepsilon}
as follows:

S_{\varepsilon}=\left\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}&X\succeq 0\,,\,\tr(X)=1\,,\\
&\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}+\varepsilon\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\end{array}\right.\right\}\,.

(38)

The following lemma clarifies the interpretation of the output from the Hamiltonian updates algorithm:

Lemma 5

(Accuracy)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Assume that
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\eta\in\{0,1\}
and
X_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
be the output of Algorithm
1
. Then:

1.

If
\eta=0
,
S_{0}=\emptyset
.

2.

If
\eta=1
, then
X_{\varepsilon}\in S_{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 6

(Complexity of computing a dense matrix exponential to error
\varepsilon
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
and let
\|\cdot\|
denote a submultiplicative operator norm. For any target accuracy
0<\varepsilon<1
, one can compute an approximation
\widetilde{E}
with

\|\widetilde{E}-e^{A}\|\leq\varepsilon

using

O\!\big(N^{\omega}[\log\|A\|+\log(1/\varepsilon)]\big)

operations on a classical computer, where
\omega\approx 2.373
is the exponent of matrix multiplication.

Proof.

The scaling-and-squaring method proceeds as follows:

1.

Scale
A
by
2^{-s}
, with
s=\lceil\log_{2}(\|A\|/\theta)\rceil
, so that
B=2^{-s}A
satisfies
\|B\|\leq\theta
for some small fixed
\theta>0
.

2.

Compute a Padé (or truncated Taylor) approximant
\widetilde{R}_{m}(B)
of
e^{B}
of degree
m
such that
\|\widetilde{R}_{m}(B)-e^{B}\|\leq\varepsilon_{0}=O(\varepsilon)
. Analytic approximation theory guarantees that
m=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
suffices.

3.

Recover
e^{A}
via repeated squaring:
\widetilde{E}=(\widetilde{R}_{m}(B))^{2^{s}}
.

The dominant costs are
m
matrix multiplications for the approximant and
s
squarings, each costing
O(N^{\omega})
operations. Substituting
m=O(\log(1/\varepsilon))
and
s=O(\log\|A\|)
yields the stated complexity.
∎

Lemma 7

(Classical complexity of Hamitonian updates for semidefinite feasibility)
.

The complexity of Algorithm
1
, up to polylogarithmic factors, is as follows:

•

On a classical computer, the algorithm takes

\widetilde{O}\Big((N^{\omega}+MsN)\varepsilon^{-2}\Big),

where
\omega\approx 2.373
and
s
is the maximum number of nonzero entries per row of
A_{i}
s.

•

On a quantum computer, the algorithm requires

O(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4})

(39)

operations.

Proof.

The quantum complexity is derived from the results of Apeldoorn and Gilyén
[
43
]
.
Let us prove the classical complexity based on Lemma
6
.
On a classical computer, each iteration
t=1,\dots,T
requires:

1.

Computing
P^{(t)}=W^{(t)}/\tr(W^{(t)})
, which costs
O(N^{2})
.

2.

Checking feasibility
\tr(A_{i}P^{(t)})
for
i=1,\dots,M
, costing
O(MsN)
due to the sparsity of
A_{i}
.

3.

Updating
W^{(t+1)}=\exp(\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sum_{\tau=1}^{t}M^{(\tau)})
, which is a dense matrix exponential. By Lemma
6
, this costs
O(N^{\omega}\log(1/\varepsilon))
.

Multiplying the per-iteration cost by
T=O(\log N/\varepsilon^{2})
gives the total complexity

O\Big([N^{\omega}\log(1/\varepsilon)+MsN]\frac{\log N}{\varepsilon^{2}}\Big).

Hence the result follows.
∎

3.4
Binary search using Hamiltonian updates for semidefinite programs with affine equality constraints

In the following lemma, we provide a lower bound for the evaluation of the objective function of a semidefinite program at a positive semidefinite matrix, in terms of the violation of the affine constraints at that matrix:

Lemma 8
.

Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
,
C\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and let
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{S}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
be a linear operator defined by

\mathcal{A}(Y)=(\tr(A_{1}Y),\dots,\tr(A_{M}Y))\,,

(40)

where
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Consider primal-dual semidefinite programs:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{Y}&\tr(CY)\\
\text{s.t.}&Y\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\mathcal{A}Y=b\,,\end{array}

(41)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau^{\star}=\sup\limits_{\xi}&b^{\top}\xi\\
\text{s.t.}&\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\,,\,C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi\succeq 0\,,\end{array}

(42)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\top}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to\mathbb{S}^{N}
is the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{A}
, given by

\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}A_{i}\,.

(43)

Assume strong duality holds for problems (
41
)-(
42
):
\lambda^{\star}=\tau^{\star}
, and both problems admit solutions
Y^{\star}
and
\xi^{\star}
, respectively.
Then, for all
X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
, the following holds:

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.

(44)

Proof.

Set
Z^{\star}=C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}
.
Then
Z^{\star}\succeq 0
.
The Lagrangian has the form

L(Y,Z,\xi)=\tr(CY)-\tr(ZY)-\xi^{\top}(\mathcal{A}X-b)\,.

(45)

By the Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) conditions, we have

0=\frac{\partial L}{\partial Y}(Y^{\star},Z^{\star},\xi^{\star})=C-Z^{\star}-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}\,.

(46)

and

\tr(Z^{\star}Y^{\star})=0\,.

(47)

Let
X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
.
By (
46
),
C=Z^{\star}+\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tr(CX)-\lambda^{\star}=&\tr(C(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr((Z^{\star}+\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star})(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr(Z^{\star}X)+\tr(\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr(Z^{\star}X)+\xi^{\star\top}\mathcal{A}(X-Y^{\star})\\
\geq&\xi^{\star\top}(\mathcal{A}X-b)\,.\end{array}

(48)

The first equality follows from
\tr(CY^{\star})=\lambda^{\star}
, the third equality is due to (
47
), and the last inequality is based on the positive semidefiniteness of
Z^{\star}
and
X
.
From this, we deduce:

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.

(49)

∎

Consider the following semidefinite program with trace one:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(CX)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(X)=1\,,\\
&\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\end{array}

(50)

where
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, are given.
Define the set
S^{(\lambda)}
as

S^{(\lambda)}=\left\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}&\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\\
&\tr(CX)\leq\lambda\,,\,\tr(X)=1\end{array}\right.\right\}\,.

(51)

We propose the following algorithm to numerically solve the SDP (
50
):

Algorithm 2
.

Binary search using Hamiltonian updates

•

Input:
\lambda_{\min}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda_{\max}>\lambda_{\min}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i=1,\dots,M
.

•

Output:
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Set
\underline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda_{\min}
,
\overline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda_{\max}
and
T=\left\lceil\log_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\rceil
.

2.

For
t=0,\dots,T-1
, do

(a)

Set
\lambda_{t}=\frac{\underline{\lambda}_{t}+\overline{\lambda}_{t}}{2}
.

(b)

Run Algorithm
1
(Hamiltonian updates) to test the feasibility of
S^{(\lambda_{t})}
(defined in (
51
)) and obtain
\eta_{t}\in\{0,1\}
and
X_{\varepsilon}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

(c)

Based on
\eta_{t}
:

i.

If
\eta_{t}=0
, set
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{t}
.

ii.

If
\eta_{t}=1
, set
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\underline{\lambda}_{t}
,
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=X_{\varepsilon}^{(t)}
.

Define the set:

S^{(\lambda)}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}&\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}+\varepsilon\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\\
&\tr(-A_{i}X)\leq-b_{i}+\varepsilon\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\\
&\tr(CX)\leq\lambda+\varepsilon\,,\,\tr(X)=1\end{array}\right.\right\}\,.

(52)

Note that
S^{(\lambda)}_{0}=S^{(\lambda)}
.

The following lemma provides a necessary and sufficient condition for
S^{(\lambda)}
to be nonempty:

Lemma 9
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value of the semidefinite program in (
50
).
Assume that the problem (
50
) has an optimal solution.
Define
S^{(\lambda)}
as in (
51
).
Then,

S^{(\lambda)}\neq\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda\geq\lambda^{\star}\,.

(53)

Proof.

By assumption, there exists an optimal solution
X^{\star}
to the semidefinite program (
50
).
If
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\star}
, then
\lambda\geq\lambda^{\star}=\tr(CX^{\star})
, and
X^{\star}
is a feasible solution to the problem (
50
).
Thus
X^{\star}\in S^{(\lambda)}
, which shows that
S^{(\lambda)}
is nonempty.
Conversely, if
S^{(\lambda)}\neq\emptyset
, there exists
Y\in S^{(\lambda)}
.
This implies that
Y
is a feasible solution to semidefinite program (
50
), and thus
\lambda\geq\tr(CY)\geq\lambda^{\star}
.
Therefore, the result follows.
∎

We present the basic properties of the values returned by binary search in the following two lemmas:

Lemma 10
.

Let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
and
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequences generated by Algorithm
2
.
Then, for
t=0,\dots,T-1
, the following holds:

\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}-\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t})\,.

(54)

Proof.

Let
t\in\{0,\dots,T-1\}
be fixed and consider Step 2 (c) of Algorithm
2
.

•

If
\eta_{t}=0
, then
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{t}
, which gives

\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}-\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\lambda_{t}=\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\frac{\underline{\lambda}_{t}+\overline{\lambda}_{t}}{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t})\,.

(55)

•

If
\eta_{t}=1
, then
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\underline{\lambda}_{t}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
, which gives

\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}-\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}=\frac{\underline{\lambda}_{t}+\overline{\lambda}_{t}}{2}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t})\,.

(56)

Thus, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 11
.

Let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
and
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequences generated by Algorithm
2
.
Then, the following statements hold:

1.

For
t=0,\dots,T-1
, the sequence satisfies

\lambda_{\min}\leq\underline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}\leq\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}\leq\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\lambda_{\max}\,.

(57)

2.

For
t=0,\dots,T
, we have
\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}=\frac{1}{2^{t}}(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})
. In particular,

\overline{\lambda}_{T}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=\frac{1}{2^{T}}(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})\leq\varepsilon\,.

(58)

Proof.

The first statement is straightforward to prove, as it follows directly from the binary search update rules.
Now, let us prove the second statement.
By Lemma
10
, by induction, we have

\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{\lambda}_{t-1}-\underline{\lambda}_{t-1})=\dots=\frac{1}{2^{t}}(\overline{\lambda}_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{0})=\frac{1}{2^{t}}(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})\,.

(59)

Thus, the result follows.
∎

The following lemma establishes a lower bound on the gap between the optimal value of the SDP and the value returned by the binary search using Hamiltonian updates:

Lemma 12
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
50
), and assume that problem (
50
) has an optimal solution.
Additionally, let
\lambda_{\min}\leq\lambda^{\star}
, and let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequence generated by Algorithm
2
.
Then for all
t=0,\dots,T
,

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}\,.

(60)

Proof.

We will prove the inequality (
60
) by induction on
t
.

•

Base case (
t=0
): From the initial setup, we have
\underline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda_{\min}
, so
\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda^{\star}-\lambda_{\min}\geq 0
, which satisfies the inequality (
60
) for
t=0
.

•

Inductive step:
Assume that the inequality (
60
) holds for
t=r\leq T-1
, i.e.,
\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{r}>0
.
We claim that (
60
) holds with
t=r+1
.
Indeed,
Consider Step 2 (c) of Algorithm
2
with
t=r
.

–

If
\eta_{r}=0
, then Lemma
5
states that
S^{(\lambda_{r})}=S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{0}=\emptyset
, which implies from Lemma
9
that
\lambda^{\star}>\lambda_{r}
. Thus, we have
\lambda^{\star}>\lambda_{r}=\underline{\lambda}_{r+1}
,
ensuring that the inequality holds for
t=r+1
.

–

If
\eta_{r}=1
, then by the inductive hypothesis,
\underline{\lambda}_{r+1}=\underline{\lambda}_{r}\leq\lambda^{\star}
, so the inequality again holds.

Thus, by induction, the inequality (
60
) holds for all
t=0,\dots,T
.
∎

The following lemma provides upper bounds on the gap between the optimal value of the SDP and the value returned by the binary search using Hamiltonian updates:

Lemma 13
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
50
) and assume
\lambda_{\max}\geq\lambda^{\star}
.
Further, assume the following equality holds:

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(61)

Assume strong duality holds for problem (
61
) and its dual (
42
), which admits a solution
\xi^{\star}
.
Let
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequence generated in Algorithm
2
.
Then for
t=0,\dots,T
, we have the following bound:

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\varepsilon(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\,.

(62)

Proof.

We will prove the result by induction, using Lemma
8
.

•

Base case: For
t=0
, we have

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda^{\star}-\lambda_{\max}\leq 0\leq\varepsilon\left(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)\,.

(63)

Thus (
62
) holds for
t=0
.

•

Inductive Step:
Assume that (
62
) holds for some
t=r\leq T-1
.
We now show that it holds for
t=r+1
.
Consider Step 2 (c) of Algorithm
2
with
t=r
.

–

Case 1:
\eta_{r}=0
. In this case, we have
\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{r}
. By the induction assumption, it follows that

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{r}\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon\left(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)\,.

(64)

–

Case 2:
\eta_{r}=1
.
By Lemma
5
, we know that
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
8
, we obtain

\tr(CX_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})\geq\lambda^{\star}-\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\max\limits_{i=1,\dots,M}|\tr(A_{i}X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})-b_{i}|\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\,.

(65)

Since
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
|\tr(A_{i}X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})-b_{i}|\leq\varepsilon
for all
i
and the last inequality holds.
Therefore, we have the following estimate:

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}+\varepsilon=\lambda_{r}+\varepsilon\geq\tr(CX_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\,.

(66)

The first inequality is based on
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
.
It implies that

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\,.

(67)

Combining both cases, we conclude that

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}\leq\varepsilon\left(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)\,.

(68)

Thus, by induction, (
62
) holds for all
t=0,\dots,T
.
∎

Remark 1
.

Condition (
61
) implies that the constraint
\tr(X)=1
in problem (
50
) can be omitted without affecting the optimal value of the problem.

The following lemma establishes the accuracy of the approximate optimal value for the SDP (
50
), as obtained through binary search using Hamiltonian updates:

Lemma 14

(Accuracy)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Assume that problem (
50
) has an optimal solution.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
50
), and let
\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.
Further, assume the following equality holds:

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(69)

Assume strong duality holds for problem (
69
) and its dual (
42
), which admits a solution
\xi^{\star}
.
Let
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be the value returned by Algorithm
2
.
Then, the following convergence result holds:

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon(2+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\,.

(70)

Proof.

From Lemma
12
, we have
0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
.
From Lemma
13
, it follows that
\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}<\varepsilon\left(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)
.
By the second statement of Lemma
11
, we also have
\overline{\lambda}_{T}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon
.
Combining these results, we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rl}0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=&(\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T})+(\overline{\lambda}_{T}-\underline{\lambda}_{T})\\[5.0pt]
\leq&\varepsilon\left(1+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)+\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)\,.\end{array}

(71)

Thus, the statement follows.
∎

The following lemma, which summarizes the classical and quantum complexities of binary search via Hamiltonian updates, is a consequence of Lemma
7
.

Lemma 15

(Complexity)
.

The complexity of running Algorithm
2
is as follows:

•

On a classical computer, the algorithm takes

O((N^{\omega}+MsN)\varepsilon^{-2})

(72)

operations, where
N
is the size of the matrix,
M
is the number of affine constraints,
\varepsilon
is the desired accuracy,
\omega\approx 2.373
, and
s
is the maximum number of nonzero entries in any row of the matrices
C
and
A_{i}
(for
i=1,\dots,M
).

•

On a quantum computer, the algorithm requires

O(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4})

(73)

operations.

4
Quantum algorithms for solving sum-of-squares relaxations of polynomial optimization

4.1
Unconstrained polynomial optimization

We now turn to how to actually solve the sums-of-squares relaxations of polynomial optimization problems using quantum methods.
To build up to our complexity and accuracy guarantees, we start with the simplest setting—unconstrained polynomial optimization, where the structure of the relaxation and its SDP formulation can be more easily analysed.
Let
f=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}f_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, and consider the problem

f^{\star}=\inf\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}f(x)\,,

(74)

which seeks the global minimum of
f
.

The sum-of-squares (SOS) relaxation of order
k
for this problem is formulated as

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&f-\lambda\in\Sigma[x]_{k}\,,\end{array}

(75)

where
\Sigma[x]_{k}
denotes the set of SOS polynomials of degree at most
2k
.
This relaxation satisfies the chain of inequalities
\lambda_{k}\leq\lambda_{k+1}\leq f^{\star}
.

The SOS relaxation (
75
) can be equivalently reformulated as the following semidefinite program (SDP):

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G\succeq 0\,,\,f-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}\,,\end{array}

(76)

where
v_{k}
is the vector of monomials up to degree
k
.

The dual of (
76
) is given by

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau_{k}=\min\limits_{y}&L_{y}(f)\\
\text{s.t.}&y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\,,\\
&M_{k}(y)\succeq 0\,,\,y_{0}=1\,,\\
\end{array}

(77)

where
M_{k}(y)
is the moment matrix of order
k
associated with
y
, and
L_{y}
is the Riesz linear functional on
{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
.

According to Lemma
2
,
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
, and problem (
76
) admits an optimal solution.

We make the following coefficient scaling assumption on the objective polynomial
f
of the polynomial optimization (
74
).This will turn out to be crucial to ensure that we can bound the trace of feasible points of the SDP.

Assumption 1
.

There exist
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and positive measure
\mu
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
and

\frac{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{k}(\mu))}\leq 1\,.

(78)

Remark 2
.

If we have a positive lower bound
\delta
for the smallest eigenvalue of the moment matrix of order
k
associated with some measure
\mu
, the coefficients of any polynomial
f
and scalar
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
can always be rescaled to produce a new polynomial
\tilde{f}
and new scalar
\underline{\tilde{\lambda}}
that satisfy Assumption
1
.
To see this, let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
,
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
and
0<\delta\leq\lambda_{\min}(M_{k}(\mu))
.
Set
\tau=\frac{\delta}{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}
.
Since
f\geq f^{\star}\geq\lambda_{k}\geq\underline{\lambda}
, we have
f-\underline{\lambda}\geq 0
, which implies
\tau>0
.
Define
\tilde{f}=\tau f
and
\tilde{\underline{\lambda}}=\tau\underline{\lambda}
.
Now consider the rescaled sum-of-squares relaxation. By definition:

\tilde{\lambda}_{k}=\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G\succeq 0\,,\,\tilde{f}-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}\}\,.

(79)

Substituting
\tilde{f}=\tau f
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tilde{\lambda}_{k}=&\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G\succeq 0\,,\,\tau f-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G\succeq 0\,,\,f-\frac{\lambda}{\tau}=v_{k}^{\top}(\frac{1}{\tau}G)v_{k}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\sup\{\tau\tilde{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists\tilde{G}\succeq 0\,,\,f-\tilde{\lambda}=v_{k}^{\top}\tilde{G}v_{k}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\tau\sup\{\tilde{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists\tilde{G}\succeq 0\,,\,f-\tilde{\lambda}=v_{k}^{\top}\tilde{G}v_{k}\}\\
=&\tau\lambda_{k}\,.\end{array}

(80)

Thus,
\tilde{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
,
\tilde{\underline{\lambda}}\leq\tau\lambda_{k}=\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
, and

\frac{\int(\tilde{f}-\tilde{\underline{\lambda}})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{k}(\mu))}\leq\frac{\int(\tilde{f}-\tilde{\underline{\lambda}})d\mu}{\delta}=\frac{\tau\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\delta}=1\,.

(81)

In Appendix
C
, we provide an explicit value of
\delta
when
\mu
is the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]^{n}
(see Proposition
1
).

The following lemma states that, under the coefficient scaling Assumption
1
, the trace of the Gram matrix that solves the sum-of-squares relaxation is upper bounded by one:

Lemma 16
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
, and suppose Assumption
1
holds.
Let
G^{\star}
be an optimal solution to the semidefinite program (
76
).
Then,
\tr(G^{\star})\leq 1
.

Proof.

From the relation
f-\lambda_{k}=v_{k}G^{\star}v_{k}^{\top}
, we have

\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu\geq\int(f-\lambda_{k})d\mu=\tr(G^{\star}M_{k}(\mu))\geq\lambda_{\min}(M_{k}(\mu))\tr(G^{\star})\,.

(82)

From property
M_{k}(\mu)\succeq 0
, it follows that:

\tr(G^{\star})\leq\frac{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(M_{k}(\mu))}\leq 1\,,

(83)

where the final inequality follows from Assumption
1
.
Thus, the result is proven.
∎

To transform the sum-of-squares relaxation (
76
) into the standard SDP form (
50
), we introduce the family of symmetric matrices
(B^{(\gamma)})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}
, defined as follows.

Definition 7
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
.
The
coefficient-matching matrix

B^{(\gamma)}
is the symmetric matrix indexed by
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}
, defined entrywise by

B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}\;:=\;\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\alpha+\beta=\gamma,\\[2.0pt]
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(84)

It is straightforward to show that
B^{(\gamma)}=M_{k}(z)
with
z_{\alpha}=1
if
\alpha=\gamma
and
z_{\alpha}=0
else,
M_{k}(y)=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}B^{(\gamma)}
,
v_{k}v_{k}^{\top}=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}x^{\gamma}B^{(\gamma)}
and that

B^{(0)}=\diag(1,0,\dots,0)
.

Example 1
.

For
n=1
and
k=2
, we have

v_{k}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
x\\
x^{2}\end{bmatrix}\,,\,v_{k}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}1&x&x^{2}\end{bmatrix}\,,\,v_{k}v_{k}^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}1&x&x^{2}\\
x&x^{2}&x^{3}\\
x^{2}&x^{3}&x^{4}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(85)

Thus, we can express
v_{k}v_{k}^{\top}
as a sum of the form

v_{k}v_{k}^{\top}=B^{(0)}+xB^{(1)}+x^{2}B^{(2)}+x^{3}B^{(3)}+x^{4}B^{(4)}

(86)

where the matrices
B^{(i)}
are given by

\begin{array}[]{rl}&B^{(0)}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\,,\,B^{(1)}=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\,,\,B^{(2)}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&0\end{bmatrix}\,,\\[18.0pt]
&B^{(3)}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&1&0\end{bmatrix}\,,\,B^{(4)}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}\,.\end{array}

(87)

The following properties of
B^{(\gamma)}
will be useful later for analyzing the accuracy of the approximate value returned by the binary search using Hamiltonian updates, applied to solve the sum-of-squares relaxation (
76
):

Lemma 17
.

Let
B^{(\gamma)}
be defined as in (
84
). The following properties hold:

(i)

If
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}=1
, then
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}=0
, for all
\beta^{\prime}\neq\beta
, and
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}=1
implies
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha^{\prime},\beta}=0
, for all
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\alpha
.

(ii)

If
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}=1
, then
B^{(\gamma^{\prime})}_{\alpha,\beta}=0
, for all
\gamma^{\prime}\neq\gamma
.

(iii)

B^{(\gamma)2}
is a diagonal matrix with diagonal entries that are either
1
or
0
. Specifically,

B^{(\gamma)2}=\diag(0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)\,.

(88)

(iv)

-I\preceq B^{(\gamma)}\preceq I
.

(v)

\tr(B^{(\gamma)}B^{(\gamma^{\prime})})\begin{cases}=0&\text{if }\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}\,,\\
\geq 1&\text{else.}\end{cases}

(vi)

The matrices
B^{(\gamma)}
, for
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}
, are linearly independent in
\mathbb{S}^{\binom{n+k}{n}}
.

Proof.

(
i
) Suppose
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}=1=B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta^{\prime}}
for some
\beta^{\prime}\neq\beta
. Then,
\alpha+\beta=\gamma=\alpha+\beta^{\prime}
, which implies
\beta=\beta^{\prime}
, a contradiction.
A similar argument holds for the columns due to the symmetry of
B^{(\gamma)}
.

(
ii
) Suppose
B^{(\gamma)}_{\alpha,\beta}=1=B^{(\gamma^{\prime})}_{\alpha,\beta}
for some
\gamma^{\prime}\neq\gamma
. Then,
\gamma=\alpha+\beta=\gamma^{\prime}
, implying
\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

The following implications hold:
(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
iv
), and (
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
v
)
\Rightarrow
(
vi
).
∎

In addition, we show in Appendix
F

how to explicittly generate block encodings for
B^{(\gamma)}
by applying
O(n\log(k))
gates, showing that this is not a bottleneck for the application of the methods.

We now present our first main algorithm, which provides an approximation of the optimal value to the SOS relaxation of order
k
.

Algorithm 3
.

Unconstrained polynomial optimization

•

Input:
\varepsilon>0
,
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
of
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
.

•

Output:
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
G_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Convert relaxation to standard semidefinite program:

(a)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{M}\}
, with
M=|{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}|=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
.

(b)

Define
\tilde{C}=B^{(0)}
,
\tilde{A}_{i}=B^{(\gamma_{i})}
,
b_{i}=f_{\gamma_{i}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and set
\tilde{N}=\binom{n+k}{n}
.

(c)

Define
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
,
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and set
N=\tilde{N}+1
.

2.

Initialize search bounds: Set
\lambda_{\max}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}
and
\lambda_{\min}=f_{0}-f(a)
.

3.

Run Algorithm
2
(Binary search using Hamiltonian updates)
to get
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

4.

Extract approximate results: Set
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
and
G_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
.

Remark 3
.

In Step
1a
of Algorithm
3
, elements in
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}
can be indexed using lexicographic order.

The following lemma demonstrates that SOS relaxations can be reformulated as standard-form SDPs satisfying the bounded-trace property.

Lemma 18
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, and suppose that Assumption
1
holds.
Let
\tilde{C}
,
\tilde{A}_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
\tilde{N}
be generated by Algorithm
3
.
Then the problem in (
76
) is equivalent to:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\inf\limits_{G}&\tr(\tilde{C}G)\\
\text{s.t.}&G\in\mathbb{S}^{\tilde{N}}_{+}\,,\,\tr(G)\leq 1\,,\\
&\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(89)

Moreover, this is further equivalent to the following form, where the trace constraint is removed:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\inf\limits_{G}&\tr(\tilde{C}G)\\
\text{s.t.}&G\in\mathbb{S}^{\tilde{N}}_{+}\,,\\
&\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(90)

Proof.

By Lemma
16
, the problem in (
76
) is equivalent to:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G\succeq 0\,,\,\tr(G)\leq 1\,,\,f-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}\,.\end{array}

(91)

To express this in standard SDP form, let
G=(G_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}
. Following the steps in SOS testing, we have:

f-\lambda=v_{k}^{\top}Gv_{k}\\
\Leftrightarrow\begin{cases}f_{0}-\lambda=G_{0,0}=\tr(B^{(0)}G)\,,\\
f_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma\end{array}}G_{\alpha,\beta}=\tr(B^{(\gamma)}G)\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{cases}

(92)

Rewriting, we find:

\begin{cases}\lambda=f_{0}-\tr(B^{(0)}G)\,,\\
f_{\gamma_{i}}=\tr(B^{(\gamma_{i})}G)\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\end{cases}

(93)

which leads to:

\begin{cases}\lambda=f_{0}-\tr(\tilde{C}G)\,,\\
b_{i}=\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{cases}

(94)

Thus, (
91
) can be reformulated as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{G}&f_{0}-\tr(\tilde{C}G)\\
\text{s.t.}&G\in\mathbb{S}^{\tilde{N}}_{+}\,,\,\tr(G)\leq 1\,,\\
&\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,{M}\,.\end{array}

(95)

This is equivalent to (
89
).

Finally, we observe that removing the trace constraint

\tr(G)\leq 1

does not affect the optimal value or solutions of (
91
), as shown in the proof of Lemma
16
.
Hence, the problem in (
76
) is also equivalent to (
90
).
Thus, the result follows.
∎

The next lemma shows that an SDP with the bounded-trace property can be transformed into an equivalent SDP with the constant-trace property by appropriately lifting its matrix variable.

Lemma 19
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
and suppose that Assumption
1
holds.
Define
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Let
C
,
b_{i}
,
A_{i}
(for
i=1,\dots,M
),
\tilde{N}
, and
N
be generated by Algorithm
3
.
Then problem (
50
) holds, and it is equivalent to problem (
89
) in the following sense:

•

If
G^{\star}
is an optimal solution to (
89
), then the matrix
\diag(G^{\star},c)
, with
c=1-\tr(G^{\star})
, is an optimal solution to (
50
).

•

Conversely, if
X^{\star}=(X^{\star}_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}
is an optimal solution to (
50
), then the leading principal submatrix of
X^{\star}
,
\tilde{X}^{\star}=(X^{\star}_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
, is an optimal solution to (
89
).

Furthermore, the following equality holds:

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(96)

Proof.

We first prove that (
50
) holds. Let
X
be a feasible solution to (
50
), and define
\tilde{X}=(X_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
. Since
X\succeq 0
, , it follows that
\tilde{X}\succeq 0
. Additionally, because
\tr(X)=1
, we have
\tr(\tilde{X})\leq 1
.

From Lemma
18
, (
89
) holds.
Moreover, since
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, we have
b_{i}=\tr(A_{i}X)=\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}\tilde{X})
, which implies that
\tilde{X}
is a feasible solution to (
89
).
Thus,
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}\leq\tr(\tilde{C}\tilde{X})=\tr(CX)
, using
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
.

Now, let
G^{\star}
be an optimal solution to problem (
89
).
Define
Y=\diag(G^{\star},c)
, where
c=1-\tr(G^{\star})
.
Since
\tr(G^{\star})\leq 1
, it follows that
c\geq 0
, implying
Y\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
.
Additionally, we have
\tr(Y)=\tr(G^{\star})+c=1
, and and for all
i=1,\dots,M
,

b_{i}=\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G^{\star})=\tr(AY)
.
This shows that
Y
is a feasible solution to (
50
).
Furthermore,
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\tr(\tilde{C}G^{\star})=\tr(CY)
since
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
and
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, for all
i=1,\dots,M
.
Thus, (
50
) holds, and
Y
is an optimal solution to (
50
).

Next, let
X^{\star}=(X^{\star}_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}
be an optimal solution to (
50
), and define
\tilde{X}^{\star}=(X^{\star}_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
.
Then
\tilde{X}^{\star}\in\mathbb{S}^{\tilde{N}}
, and
\tr(\tilde{X}^{\star})=\tr(X^{\star})-X^{\star}_{NN}=1-X^{\star}_{NN}\leq 1
.
Additionally, for all
i=1,\dots,M
,
b_{i}=\tr(A_{i}X^{\star})=\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}\tilde{X}^{\star})
, and
f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{\star}=\tr(CX^{\star})=\tr(\tilde{C}\tilde{X}^{\star})
, using
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
and
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
,
i=1,\dots,M
.
Therefore,
\tilde{X}^{\star}
is an optimal solution to (
89
).

By the last statement of Lemma
18
, the equality (
96
) holds.
Hence the result follows.
∎

The following lemma establishes key properties of the input data in the standard form of SDPs. These properties will later enable the application of the error bound for Hamiltonian updates.

Lemma 20
.

Let
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
, and let
C
,
b_{i}
,
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N
,
\lambda_{\max}
,
\lambda_{\min}
be generated by Algorithm
3
.
The following properties hold:

(i)

\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.

(ii)

-I_{N}\preceq C\preceq I_{N}
,
-I_{N}\preceq A_{i}\preceq I_{N}
, for all
i=1,\dots,M
.

(iii)

The maximum number of nonzero entries in each row of
C
and
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, is
s=1
.

Proof.

(
i
) Since
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}\leq f^{\star}\leq f(a)
, it follows that

\lambda_{\min}=f_{0}-f(a)\leq f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{\star}\leq f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}=\lambda_{\max}\,.

(97)

(
ii
) By construction,
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)=\diag(B^{(0)},0)
, and
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)=\diag(B^{(\gamma_{i})},0)
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
By Lemma
17
(
iv
), it follows that
-I_{N}\preceq C\preceq I_{N}
,
-I_{N}\preceq A_{i}\preceq I_{N}
.

(
iii
) By Lemma
17
(
i
), each row of
C
and
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, contains at most
s=1
nonzero entry.
∎

The lemma below characterizes the correspondence between the optimal solutions for the moment relaxation and the equivalent standard-form semidefinite program.

Lemma 21
.

Let
C
,
A_{i},b_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
N
be generated by Algorithm
3
.
Assume that strong duality holds for problems (
76
)-(
77
),as well as for problem (
96
) and its dual (
42
).
Let
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, and define
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
such that
y_{0}=1
and
y_{\gamma_{i}}=-\xi_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Then
y
is an optimal solution to the moment relaxation (
77
) if and only if
\xi
is an optimal solution to problem (
42
).

Proof.

By definition of
b,\tilde{C},\tilde{A}_{i}
, we have
L_{y}(f)=y_{0}f_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{i}}f_{\gamma_{i}}=f_{0}-b^{\top}\xi
and

M_{k}(y)=y_{0}B^{(0)}+\sum_{i=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{i}}B^{(\gamma_{i})}=\tilde{C}-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}\tilde{A}_{i}
, which implies
\diag(M_{k}(y),0)=C-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}A_{i}=C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi
.
From this, the following equivalences hold:

\begin{array}[]{rl}&y\text{ is an optimal solution to problem \eqref{eq:sdp.mom.relaxation}}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&M_{k}(y)\succeq 0\text{ and }L_{y}(f)=\tau_{k}=\lambda_{k}\qquad\text{ (by strong duality)}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi\succeq 0\text{ and }b^{\top}\xi=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{\star}=\tau^{\star}\qquad\text{ (by strong duality)}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&\xi\text{ is an optimal solution for \eqref{eq:dual.no.tr.cons}.}\end{array}

(98)

∎

We now present our first main theorem, which establishes an error bound for the approximation produced by our first main algorithm.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
, and suppose that Assumption
1
holds.
Assume strong duality holds for primal-dual semidefinite relaxations (
76
)-(
77
).
Let
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm
3
.
If
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
and polynomial optimization problem (
74
) has an optimal solution in
\ell_{1}
-ball
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,\|x\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq r\}
for some
r\in(0,1)
, then

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-f^{\star}\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\frac{r}{1-r}\right)\,.

(99)

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Since strong duality holds for problems (
76
)-(
77
), it holds for problem (
96
) and its dual (
42
) by Lemma
19
.

Assume
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
and polynomial optimization problem (
74
) has an optimal solution
x^{\star}
satisfying
\|x^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq r
for some
r\in(0,1)
.
Let
y^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be the truncated moment sequence with respect to the Dirac measure
\delta_{x^{\star}}
, i.e.,
y^{\star}_{\alpha}=\int x^{\alpha}d\delta_{x^{\star}}=x^{\star\alpha}
.

We claim that
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
77
).
First, observe that
y^{\star}_{0}=(x^{\star})^{0}=1
and
M_{k}(y^{\star})=v_{k}(x^{\star})v_{k}(x^{\star})^{\top}\succeq 0
.
Then
y^{\star}
is a feasible solution for problem (
77
).
Additionally,
L_{y^{\star}}(f)=\int fd\delta_{x^{\star}}=f(x^{\star})=f^{\star}=\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
.
The last equality follows from strong duality. Therefore,
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
77
).
By Lemma
21
, this implies
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
, with
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
, is an optimal solution to problem (
42
).

By Lemma
20
, (
i
) and (
ii
),
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
,
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.
By Lemma
19
, (
96
) holds.
From these, by Lemma
14
,
0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon(2+\|\tilde{y}^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})
.
Recall that
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
. Substituting, we get:

\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-f^{\star}
.
Thus,

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-f^{\star}\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\|\tilde{y}^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\right)\,.

(100)

Next, we bound the
\ell_{1}
-norm of
\tilde{y}^{\star}
.
By the multinomial theorem,

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j}=\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}x^{\gamma}\,,\,\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,,\,\forall j\in{\mathbb{N}}\,.

(101)

Setting
x_{i}=|x_{i}^{\star}|
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}r^{j}\geq&\|x^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}^{j}=(|x_{1}^{\star}|+\dots+|x_{n}^{\star}|)^{j}\\[5.0pt]
=&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}|x_{1}^{\star}|^{\gamma_{1}}\dots|x_{n}^{\star}|^{\gamma_{n}}\\
\geq&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}|x^{\star\gamma}|=\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}|y^{\star}_{\gamma}|\,.\end{array}

(102)

The last inequality holds because
\binom{j}{\gamma}\geq 1
for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
with
|\gamma|=j
.
Thus,

\|\tilde{y}^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}|y^{\star}_{\gamma}|=\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}|y^{\star}_{\gamma}|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2k}r^{j}\leq r\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}r^{t}=\frac{r}{1-r}\,.

(103)

From this and (
100
), the result follows.

∎

Lemma 22

(Complexity)
.

The runtime complexity of Algorithm
3
is as follows:

•

On a classical computer, it requires

O\left(\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}\binom{n+k}{n}+\binom{n+k}{n}^{\omega}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

(104)

operations, where
\omega\approx 2.373
.

•

On a quantum computer, it requires

O\left(\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\binom{n+k}{n}^{1/2}\ \right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

(105)

operations.

Proof.

The most computationally intensive part of Algorithm
3
is the execution of Algorithm
2
, which solves a semidefinite program (SDP). The SDP has
M
affine equality constraints and operates on a positive semidefinite matrix of size
N
.
By Lemma
15
, the runtime complexity for solving this SDP is:

•

On a classical computer:
O((MsN+N^{\omega})\varepsilon^{-2})
,

•

On a quantum computer:
O(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4})
,

where
s
is the maximum number of nonzero entries in any row of the data matrices
C,A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
From Lemma
20
(
iii
), we know
s=1
.
Additionally:
M=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
and
N=\binom{n+k}{n}+1
.
Substituting these values into the complexity expressions gives the result.
∎

4.2
Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

Building upon the unconstrained case, we now show similar results for inequality constrained polynomial optimization.
Let
f=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}f_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
and
g_{i}=\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}}g_{i,\alpha}x^{\alpha}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, where
g_{0}=1
.
Define
d=\lceil\deg(f)/2\rceil
and
d_{i}=\lceil\deg(g_{i})/2\rceil
,
i=0,\dots,m
.
Consider the polynomial optimization problem

f^{\star}=\inf_{x\in S(g)}f(x)\,,

(106)

where
S(g)
is the semialgebraic set defined as

S(g)=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,g_{i}(x)\geq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\}\,,

(107)

with
g=(g_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
.

The sum-of-squares (SOS) relaxation of order
k
for problem (
106
) is formulated as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,\sigma_{i}}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,\sigma_{i}\in\Sigma[x]_{k-d_{i}}\,,\,f-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sigma_{i}g_{i}\,.\end{array}

(108)

Here,
\Sigma[x]_{k-d_{i}}
denotes the cone of sum-of-squares polynomials of degree at most
2(k-d_{i})
.
It is known that

\lambda_{k}\leq\lambda_{k+1}\leq f^{\star}
.
Furthermore, if
g_{m}=R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
for some
R>0
, then
\lambda_{k}\to f^{\star}
as
k\to\infty
.

Problem (
108
) can be reformulated as a semidefinite program (SDP):

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G=\diag(G_{0},\dots,G_{m})\succeq 0\,,\\
&f-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\,.\end{array}

(109)

Here,
v_{k-d_{i}}
is the vector of monomials of degree at most
k-d_{i}
.

The duals of (
109
) reads as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau_{k}=\min\limits_{y}&L_{y}(f)\\
\text{s.t.}&y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\,,\\
&M_{k}(y)\succeq 0\,,\,y_{0}=1\,,\\
&M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y)\succeq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\,,\\
\end{array}

(110)

where
M_{k}(y)
is the moment matrix of order
k
associated with
y
,
M_{k-u}(py)
is the localizing matrix of order
k-u
associated with
y
and a polynomial
p
of degree at most
2u
, and
L_{y}
is the Riesz linear functional on
{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
.

From Lemma
1
, if
g_{1}=R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
for some
R>0
, then
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
, and problem (
110
) admits an optimal solution.
By Lemma
2
, if
S(g)
has nonempty interior, then
\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
, and problem (
109
) admits an optimal solution.

In this section, we assume that problem (
109
) has an optimal solution.

For
g=(g_{i})_{i=1,\dots,m}
with
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, the block-diagonal moment/localizing matrix of order
k
associated with a sequence
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
is defined as

D_{k}(gy)=\diag((M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y))_{i=0,\dots,m})\,,

(111)

where
g_{0}=1
and
d_{0}=0
.
For a given measure
\mu
, the block-diagonal moment/localizing matrix of order
k
associated with
\mu
is defined as
D_{k}(g\mu)=D_{k}(gy)
, where
y
is the truncated moment sequence with respect to
\mu
.

We make the following assumption:

Assumption 2
.

There exist
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and positive measure
\mu
supported on
S(g)
such that
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
and

\frac{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))}\leq 1\,.

(112)

Remark 4
.

If we have a positive lower bound
\delta
for the smallest eigenvalue of the block-diagonal moment/localizing matrix of order
k
associated with some measure
\mu
supported on
S(g)
, the coefficients of any polynomial
f
and scalar
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
can always be rescaled to produce a new polynomial
\tilde{f}
and new scalar
\underline{\tilde{\lambda}}
that satisfy Assumption
1
.
To see this, let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
,
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
and
0<\delta\leq\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))
.
Set
\tau=\frac{\delta}{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}
.
Since
f\geq f^{\star}\geq\lambda_{k}\geq\underline{\lambda}
on
S(g)
, we have
f-\underline{\lambda}\geq 0
on
S(g)
, which implies
\tau>0
.
Define
\tilde{f}=\tau f
and
\tilde{\underline{\lambda}}=\tau\underline{\lambda}
.
Now consider the rescaled sum-of-squares relaxation. By definition:

\tilde{\lambda}_{k}=\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,i=0,\dots,m\,,\,\tilde{f}-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\}\,.

(113)

Substituting
\tilde{f}=\tau f
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tilde{\lambda}_{k}=&\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,\tau f-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\sup\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists G_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,f-\frac{\lambda}{\tau}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}(\frac{1}{\tau}G)v_{k-d_{i}}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\sup\{\tau\tilde{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists\tilde{G}_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,f-\tilde{\lambda}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}\tilde{G}_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\}\\[3.0pt]
=&\tau\sup\{\tilde{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\exists\tilde{G}_{i}\succeq 0\,,\,f-\tilde{\lambda}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}\tilde{G}_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\}\\
=&\tau\lambda_{k}\,.\end{array}

(114)

Thus,
\tilde{f}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
,
\tilde{\underline{\lambda}}\leq\tau\lambda_{k}=\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
, and

\frac{\int(\tilde{f}-\tilde{\underline{\lambda}})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))}\leq\frac{\int(\tilde{f}-\tilde{\underline{\lambda}})d\mu}{\delta}=\frac{\tau\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\delta}=1\,.

(115)

In Appendix
C
, we derive a positive lower bound for the smallest eigenvalue of
D_{k}(g\mu)
in case where the coefficients of
g_{i}
are rational, and
S(g)
has nonempty interior (see Proposition
2
).

In Sec.
4.4
we discuss this assumption and show it is satisfied for portfolio optimization problems.

To express the sum-of-squares relaxation (
109
) in the standard semidefinite programming form (
50
), we define the family of matrices
(B^{(\gamma)}_{g})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}
as outlined below.

For
i=0,\dots,m
, for
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2(k-d_{i})}
, define
B^{(\eta)}_{i}
as

B^{(\eta)}_{i}=((B_{i}^{(\eta)})_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}\,,\text{ where }(B_{i}^{(\eta)})_{\alpha,\beta}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\alpha+\beta=\eta\,,\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

(116)

For
g=(g_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\subset{\mathbb{R}}[x]
with
g_{0}=1
, define

B_{g}^{(\gamma)}=\diag\left(\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)}\right)_{i=0,\dots,m}\right)\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\,.

(117)

It is not hard to show that
D_{k}(gy)=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
, for
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
.
The matrices
(B_{g}^{(\gamma)})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
are called the coefficient-matching matrices in the expression of the block-diagonal moment/localizing matrix
D_{k}(gy)
as linear combination of the entries of
y
.

We denote by
s_{g}
the maximum number of nonzero entries per row of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
.

Assumption 3
.

For
i=1,\dots,m
,
\|g_{i}\|_{\ell_{1}}=\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}|g_{i,\xi}|\leq 1
.

Remark 5
.

The coefficients of any polynomials
g=(g_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
can always be rescaled to obtain new polynomials
\tilde{g}=(\tilde{g}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
such that
S(g)=S(\tilde{g})
and
\tilde{g}
satisfies (
3
). Moreover, if the coefficients of each
g_{i}
are rational, the coefficients of
\tilde{g}_{i}
will also be rational.
This is achieved by defining
\tilde{g}_{i}=\frac{g_{i}}{\|g_{i}\|_{\ell_{1}}}
, for
i=1,\dots,m
.

Algorithm 4
.

Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

•

Input:
\varepsilon>0
,
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a\in S(g)
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
of
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(g_{i,\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}
of
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, for
i=0,\dots,m
.

•

Output:
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
G_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Convert relaxation to standard semidefinite program

(a)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}=\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{M}\}
with
\gamma_{0}=0
, and set
M=|{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}|-1=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
.

(b)

Define
b_{i}=f_{\gamma_{i}}
,
\tilde{A}_{i}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})}
, for
i=0,\dots,M
,
\tilde{C}=\tilde{A}_{0}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{0})}=B_{g}^{(0)}
,
\tilde{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}
.

(c)

Define
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
,
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N=\tilde{N}+1
.

(d)

Set
\lambda_{\min}=f_{0}-f(a)
and
\lambda_{\max}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}
.

2.

Run Algorithm
2
(Binary search using Hamiltonian updates)
to get
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

3.

Extract approximate results: Set
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
and
G_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumptions
2
and
3
are satisfied.
Assume strong duality holds for the semidefinite relaxations (
109
)-(
110
), and let
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
.
Additionally, suppose the polynomial optimization problem (
106
) has an optimal solution
x^{\star}
satisfying
\|x^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq r
, where
r\in(0,1)
.
Let
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm
4
.
Then, the following bound holds:

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\frac{r}{1-r}\right)\,.

(118)

The proof of Theorem
2
is given in Appendix
A.1.1
.

Building on Lemma
3
, we derive the following result as a corollary of Theorem
2
:

Corollary 1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
and
g_{1}=c(R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2})
for some
c>0
and
R>0
.
Suppose that Assumption
3
is satisfied.
Assume that the polynomial optimization problem (

) has an optimal solution
x^{\star}
satisfying
\|x^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq r
, where
r\in(0,1)
.
Further, assume that the second-order sufficient optimality conditions hold for problem (
106
) at every optimal solution.
Then, there exists
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
k\geq K
,
\lambda_{k}=f^{\star}
.
In this case, if Assumption
2
is met, and
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
denotes the value returned by Algorithm
4
, it holds that

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-f^{\star}\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\frac{r}{1-r}\right)\,.

(119)

Remark 6

in Corollary

are selected to ensure that Assumption

is satisfied.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for

, with

and

and

(120)

The proof of Theorem

is given in Appendix

.

Theorem 4
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
,
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Fix
r\in(0,1)
.
Assume there exist indices
i_{1},i_{2},i_{\alpha}\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, with
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
, satisfying the following:

•

g_{i_{j}}=c_{j}[r^{j}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j}]
with
c_{j}>0
for

,

•

g_{i_{\alpha}}=x^{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
.

Suppose that Assumptions
2
and
3
hold.
Further, assume that strong duality is satisfied for the semidefinite relaxations (
109
)-(
110
).
Let
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm

.

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}\leq\varepsilon\left(2+\frac{r}{1-r}\right)\,.

.

Remark 7
.

The constants

and

in Theorems

and

is satisfied.

Remark 8
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
4
, the semialgebraic set
S(g)
is contained within the simplex
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\,:\,x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\leq r\}
. However, in this case, the number of inequality constraints
m
in problem (
106
) grows at least exponentially with
n
, specifically
m\geq 2^{n}+1
.

Lemma 23

(Complexity)
.

Let
k\geq\max\{d,d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\}
.
To run Algorithm
4
, the classical computer requires

O\left(\left[s_{g}\binom{n+2k}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{\omega}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

(122)

operations, where
\omega\approx 2.373
, while the quantum computer requires

O\left(s_{g}\left[\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right)

(123)

operations, where
s_{g}
is the maximum number of nonzero entries per row of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
(defined as in (
117
)),
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
, which is not larger than the maximum number of nonzero coefficients of each
g_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
.

The proof of Lemma
23
is given in Appendix
A.1.4
.

4.3
Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization over the simplex

We relax the equality affine constraints in SDP (
109
) to obtain the following SDP:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\hat{\lambda}_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G=\diag(G_{0},\dots,G_{m})\succeq 0\,,\\[5.0pt]
&f_{0}-\lambda\geq\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i,0}(G_{i})_{0,0}\,,\\[5.0pt]
&f_{\gamma}\geq\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\xi}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\xi\end{array}}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{array}

(124)

Then
\lambda_{k}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{k}
, and if
S(g)\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
,
\hat{\lambda}_{k}\leq f^{\star}
.
Assume that
\underline{\lambda}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{k}
is given.

The duals of (
124
) reads as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\hat{\tau}_{k}=\min\limits_{y}&L_{y}(f)\\
\text{s.t.}&y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\,,\\
&M_{k}(y)\succeq 0\,,\,y_{0}=1\,,\\
&M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y)\succeq 0\,,\,i=1,\dots,m\,.\\
\end{array}

(125)

Algorithm 5
.

Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

•

Input:
\varepsilon>0
,
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a\in S(g)
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
of
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(g_{i,\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}
of
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, for
i=0,\dots,m
.

•

Output:
\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
G_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Convert relaxation to standard semidefinite program

(a)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}=\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{M}\}
with
\gamma_{0}=0
, and set
M=|{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}|-1=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
.

(b)

Define
b_{i}=f_{\gamma_{i}}
,
\tilde{A}_{i}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})}
, for
i=0,\dots,M
,
\tilde{C}=\tilde{A}_{0}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{0})}=B_{g}^{(0)}
,
\tilde{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}
.

(c)

Define
C=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
,
A_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N=\tilde{N}+1
.

(d)

Set
\lambda_{\min}=f_{0}-f(a)
and
\lambda_{\max}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}
.

2.

Run Algorithm

(Binary search using Hamiltonian updates)
to get
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

3.

Extract approximate results: Set
\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
and
G_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
.

,
