4.4
Application for portfolio optimization

Portfolio optimization is a fundamental problem in quantitative finance, where the goal is to allocate wealth among a set of assets in order to balance expected return and risk. A common model for the risk is based on the covariance matrix of asset returns. The following optimization problem considers a normalized version of the mean-variance formulation:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\eta=\min\limits_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}&\frac{1}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}z^{\top}Qz\\
\text{s.t.}&w^{\top}z\geq\kappa\,,\\
&z_{1}+\dots+z_{n}\leq\zeta\,,\\
&z_{j}\geq 0\,,\,j=1,\dots,n\,,\end{array}

(130)

where
Q=(Q_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,n}
denotes the covariance matrix of the asset returns, and
\|Q\|_{1,1}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}|Q_{ij}|
is its entrywise 1-norm used for normalization.

In theory,
Q
is positive semidefinite (psd).
However, in practice, due to sampling noise, estimation errors, or regularization (e.g., shrinkage techniques),
Q
may fail to be psd. In that case, problem (
130
) becomes nonconvex.

Throughout, we assume that
\zeta
is a constant independent of
n
and that problem (
130
) admits at least one optimal solution.

When
Q
is psd, (
130
) is a convex quadratic program, and hence a polynomial optimization problem. To the best of our knowledge, interior-point methods is the fastest known classical algorithms for solving convex quadratic programs (
130
) with complexity
O(n^{\omega+1}\log(1/\varepsilon))
,
\omega\approx 2.373
(see
[
28
]
).

In contrast, our quantum algorithm presented in the previous section can approximate the optimal value
\eta
with accuracy
\varepsilon>0
in complexity
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
when
Q
is psd and assuming we have a block encoding of
Q
in both cases.
If
Q
is not psd, the method still yields an approximate optimal value for the first-order relaxation of (
130
) with the same complexity on a quantum computer. Thus, we obtain a quantum speedup over rigorous classical algorithms in the dimension, at the cost of a worse dependency on the precision.

Next, by setting
x=\frac{r}{\zeta}z
with
r\in(0,1)
, problem (
130
) can be equivalently written as

\begin{array}[]{rl}\frac{r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}=\min\limits_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}&\frac{1}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}x^{\top}Qx\\
\text{s.t.}&\zeta w^{\top}x\geq r\kappa\\
&x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}\leq r\,,\\
&x_{j}\geq 0\,,\,j=1,\dots,n\,,\end{array}

(131)

For our method to be applicable, we must construct a measure supported on the feasible domain of problem (
131
) such that its moments can be efficiently computed. These moments will be used to verify Assumption
2
.

To this end, we select the Lebesgue measure on a small hyper-rectangular box
[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where the bounds
t_{1},t_{2}
are defined as

t_{1}=\frac{r}{3n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad t_{2}=\frac{r}{2n}\,.

(132)

Clearly,
0<t_{1}<t_{2}<\frac{r}{n}
, and we have

t_{1}+t_{2}=\frac{5r}{6n}\quad\text{ and }\quad t_{2}-t_{1}=\frac{r}{6n}\,.

(133)

It is straightforward to verify that every point in the box
[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}
satisfies the last
n+1
affine constraints of problem (
131
).
To ensure that these points also satisfy the first affine constraint, we introduce the following assumption.

Assumption 4
.

The box
[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}
lies within the feasible region of problem (
131
), i.e.,

\frac{\zeta w^{\top}x-r\kappa}{\zeta\|w\|_{1}+r\kappa}\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\,,\,\forall x\in[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}.

(134)

Figure
1
illustrates this box construction in the two-dimensional case.

If
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
and the inequality

\frac{\zeta t_{1}\|w\|_{1}-r\kappa}{\zeta\|w\|_{1}+r\kappa}\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}}

(135)

holds, the condition (
134
) is satisfied.
In particular, if
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
satisfies
n^{2}\geq\|w\|_{1}\geq n
and
\zeta-3\kappa>0
, then (
134
) holds with a constant factor independent of
n
.

t_{1}
t_{2}
t_{1}
t_{2}
\zeta w^{\top}x=r\kappa
r
r

Figure 1:
Plot of Assumption
4
.

The following theorem establishes the error bound and computational complexity of our proposed method when applied to the portfolio optimization problem.

Theorem 7
.

Consider portfolio optimization problem (
130
) with covariance matrix
Q
and optimal value
\eta\geq 0
.
Let
\lambda_{1}
denote the optimal value of the first order SOS relaxation for problem (
130
), and assume that
\lambda_{1}\geq 0
.
Suppose that Assumption
4
holds. Then, for any given accuracy parameter
\varepsilon>0
, the following statements are true:

(i)

(General case) Our Algorithm
5
returns an approximate value in the interval
[\lambda_{1},\eta+\varepsilon]
with computational complexity
O(n^{3}\varepsilon^{-2})
on a classical computer, and
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
on a quantum computer.

(ii)

(Convex case) If
Q
is positive semidefinite, our Algorithm
5
provides an approximate value in the tighter range
[\eta,\eta+\varepsilon]
with computational complexity

O(n^{3}\varepsilon^{-2})

on a classical computer, and

O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})

on a quantum computer.

Proof.

(
i
) Let
\delta=\frac{1}{152}
,
f=\frac{\delta}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}x^{\top}Qx
,
m=n+3
,
g_{j}=x_{j}
, for
j=1,\dots,n
,
g_{n+1}=\frac{1}{n+1}(r-\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j})
, and
g_{n+2}=\frac{\zeta w^{\top}x-r\kappa}{\zeta\|w\|_{1}+r\kappa}
,
g_{n+3}=\frac{1}{n^{2}+1}[r^{2}-(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j})^{2}]
.
Then problem (
131
) is equivalent to POP (
106
) with
f^{\star}=\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}\geq 0
and

S(g)=\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}\,:\,\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}\leq r\}\,.

(136)

It is not hard to verify that Assumption
3
holds.

Define
\lambda_{k}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
as in (
109
) and (
124
), respectively.
Since
S(g)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
,

\lambda_{1}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}\leq f^{\star}\,.

(137)

Let
\underline{\lambda}=0
.
Then
\lambda_{1}\geq 0=\underline{\lambda}
.
We now prove that Assumption
2
holds with
k=1
and
\mu
as a Lebesgue measure on
[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}
.
Take
y=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
as the truncated moment vector with respect to
\mu
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{rl}y_{\alpha}=&\int x^{\alpha}d\mu=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}x^{\alpha}dx=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}x_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}dx_{j}\\[10.0pt]
=&\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left.\frac{x_{j}^{\alpha_{j}+1}}{\alpha_{j}+1}\right|_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{t_{2}^{\alpha_{j}+1}-t_{1}^{\alpha_{j}+1}}{\alpha_{j}+1}\,.\end{array}

(138)

With
(e_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
being the standard basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}y_{0}=&(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}\,,\\[5.0pt]
y_{e_{j}}=&\frac{1}{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}(t_{1}+t_{2})\,,\\[5.0pt]
y_{e_{i}+e_{j}}=&\frac{1}{4}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}(t_{1}+t_{2})^{2}\,,\\[5.0pt]
y_{2e_{j}}=&\frac{1}{3}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}(t_{1}^{2}+t_{1}t_{2}+t_{2}^{2})\,,\,i\neq j\,.\end{array}

(139)

This implies

\begin{array}[]{rl}M_{1}(\mu)=&\begin{bmatrix}y_{0}&y_{e_{1}}&y_{e_{2}}&\dots&y_{e_{n}}\\
y_{e_{1}}&y_{2e_{1}}&y_{e_{1}+e_{2}}&\dots&y_{e_{1}+e_{n}}\\
y_{e_{2}}&y_{e_{2}+e_{1}}&y_{2e_{2}}&\dots&y_{e_{2}+e_{n}}\\
.&.&.&\dots&.\\
y_{e_{n}}&y_{e_{n}+e_{1}}&y_{e_{n}+e_{2}}&\dots&y_{2e_{n}}\end{bmatrix}=(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}W\,,\end{array}

(140)

where

W=\begin{bmatrix}1&a&a&\dots&a\\
a&b&a^{2}&\dots&a^{2}\\
a&a^{2}&b&\dots&a^{2}\\
.&.&.&\dots&.\\
a&a^{2}&a^{2}&\dots&b\end{bmatrix}

(141)

with
a=\frac{1}{2}(t_{1}+t_{2})
and
b=\frac{1}{3}(t_{1}^{2}+t_{1}t_{2}+t_{2}^{2})
.
By Lemma
64
, with
d=b-a^{2}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}\det(W-\lambda I_{n+1})=&\left(b-a^{2}-\lambda\right)^{n-1}\left[(1-\lambda)\left(b-a^{2}-\lambda\right)-n\left(a^{2}-(1-\lambda)a^{2}\right)\right]\\
=&\left(d-\lambda\right)^{n-1}\left[\lambda^{2}-\left(1+d+na^{2}\right)\lambda+d\right]\,.\end{array}

(142)

The zeros of the above polynomial in
\lambda
are

d=\frac{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2}}{12}=\frac{r^{2}}{432n^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1+d+na^{2}\pm\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\,,

(143)

where
\Delta=(1+d+na^{2})^{2}-4d\geq 1-4d>0
.
We have

\begin{array}[]{rl}\frac{1+d+na^{2}-\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}=&\frac{(1+d+na^{2})^{2}-\Delta}{2(1+d+na^{2}+\sqrt{\Delta})}=\frac{4d}{2(1+d+na^{2}+\sqrt{\Delta})}\geq\frac{4d}{4(1+d+na^{2})}=\frac{d}{1+d+na^{2}}\geq\frac{d}{2}=\frac{r^{2}}{864n^{2}}\,.\\
\end{array}

(144)

The first inequality is due to
\Delta\leq(1+d+na^{2})^{2}
.
The second inequality is because

d+na^{2}=\frac{r^{2}}{432n^{2}}+\frac{n(5r)^{2}}{4(6n)^{2}}=\frac{r^{2}}{432n^{2}}+\frac{25r^{2}}{144n}\leq 1\,.

(145)

This implies that
\lambda_{\min}(W)\geq\frac{r^{2}}{864n^{2}}
, yielding

\lambda_{\min}(M_{1}(\mu))\geq(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}\lambda_{\min}(W)\geq\frac{r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{864n^{2}}\,.

(146)

Next, we have that for
j=1,\dots,n
,

M_{0}(g_{j}\mu)=\int g_{j}d\mu=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}x_{j}dx=y_{e_{j}}=\frac{1}{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}(t_{1}+t_{2})=\frac{5r(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{12n}\,.

(147)

In addition, we get

\begin{array}[]{rl}M_{0}(g_{n+1}\mu)=&\int g_{n+1}d\mu=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{1}{n}(r-\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j})dx\\
\geq&\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{1}{n}(r-nt_{2})dx=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{r}{2n}dx=\frac{r(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{2n}\,,\\[15.0pt]
M_{0}(g_{n+2}\mu)=&\int g_{n+2}d\mu=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{1}{n^{2}+1}[r^{2}-(\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j})^{2}]dx\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{1}{n^{2}+1}[r^{2}-(nt_{2})^{2}]dx=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{3r^{2}}{4(n^{2}+1)}dx\\[5.0pt]
=&\frac{3r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{4(n^{2}+1)}\,,\\[10.0pt]
M_{0}(g_{n+3}\mu)=&\int g_{n+3}d\mu=\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{\zeta w^{\top}x-r\kappa}{\zeta\|w\|_{1}+r\kappa}dx\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\int_{[t_{1},t_{2}]^{n}}\frac{1}{n^{2}}dx=\frac{(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{n^{2}}\,.\end{array}

(148)

The last inequality is based on (
134
).

Since
D_{1}(g\mu)=\diag(M_{1}(\mu),M_{0}(g_{1}\mu),\dots,M_{0}(g_{n+3}\mu))
, we have

\lambda_{\min}(D_{1}(g\mu))=\min\{\lambda_{\min}(M_{1}(\mu)),M_{0}(g_{1}\mu),\dots,M_{0}(g_{n+3}\mu)\}\geq\frac{r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{864n^{2}}\,.

(149)

Besides, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu=&\frac{\delta}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}\int\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}Q_{ij}x_{i}x_{j}d\mu\\[5.0pt]
=&\frac{\delta}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}Q_{ij}y_{e_{i}+e_{j}}\\[5.0pt]
\leq&\frac{\delta}{\|Q\|_{1,1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}|Q_{ij}|b(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}\\
=&\delta b(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}=\frac{\delta(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{3}(\frac{r^{2}}{9n^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}}{6n^{2}}+\frac{r^{2}}{4n^{2}})=\frac{19\delta r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{108n^{2}}\,.\end{array}

(150)

The first inequality is because
y_{e_{i}+e_{j}}\leq b(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}
.
From these, it follows that

\frac{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(D_{1}(g\mu))}\leq\frac{19\delta r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}{108n^{2}}\times\frac{864n^{2}}{r^{2}(t_{2}-t_{1})^{n}}=152\delta=1\,.

(151)

Thus Assumption
2
holds.

Let
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm
5
with
k=1
and
\underline{\lambda}=0
.
By Theorem
6
,
\lambda_{1}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}\leq f^{\star}=\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}
and

0\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\hat{\lambda}_{1}\leq\varepsilon(2+\frac{r}{1-r})
.
This implies that
\lambda_{1}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}\leq\varepsilon(2+\frac{r}{1-r})+\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}
.
Note that
\delta=\frac{1}{152}
.
Let us fix
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
\zeta
as a constant.
We claim that
s_{g}=1
. Indeed, by definition (
117
) with
k=d_{1}=\dots=d_{m}=1
, the last
m
blocks of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
are scalar, which implies
s_{g}
is the maximum number of nonzero entries of the first block
B_{0}^{(\gamma)}=B^{(\gamma)}
. By Lemma
17
(
i
),
s_{g}=1
.

By Lemma
24
(with
s_{g}=1
,
k=d_{1}=\dots=d_{m}=1
and
m=O(n)
), the complexities of Algorithm
5
to provide a value in
[\lambda_{1},\eta+\varepsilon]
on classical and quantum computers are
O(n^{3}\varepsilon^{-2})
and
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
, respectively.

(
ii
) Now we assume that
Q
is psd.
We do the same process as in the proof of (
i
), but with
m=n+2
(the quadratic constraint
g_{n+3}
is removed).
We also have
s_{g}=1
.
We claim that

f^{\star}=\lambda_{1}=\hat{\lambda}_{1}\,.

(152)

Indeed, by Lemma
4
,
\lambda_{1}=f^{\star}
.
By (
137
), the equalities hold.

Let
\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm
5
with
k=1
and
\underline{\lambda}=0
.
By (
136
), all optimal solutions
x^{\star}
to
\min_{x\in S(g)}f(x)
satisfy
\|x^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}=x_{1}^{\star}+\dots+x_{n}^{\star}\leq r
.
By Theorem
5
,

0\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}-\hat{\lambda}_{1}\leq\varepsilon(2+\frac{r}{1-r})
.
By (
152
),
\hat{\lambda}_{1}=f^{\star}=\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}
.
This implies that
\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}\leq\hat{\lambda}_{1}^{(\varepsilon)}\leq\frac{\delta r^{2}\eta}{\zeta^{2}}+\varepsilon(2+\frac{r}{1-r})
.
Let us fix
r=\frac{1}{2}
and
\zeta
as a constant.
By Lemma
24
(with
s_{g}=1
,
k=d_{1}=\dots=d_{m}=1
and
m=O(n)
), the complexities of Algorithm
5
to provide a value in
[\eta,\eta+\varepsilon]
on classical and quantum computers are
O(n^{3}\varepsilon^{-2})
and
O(n\varepsilon^{-4}+\sqrt{n}\varepsilon^{-5})
, respectively.
∎

In
[
28
]
, Kerenidis
et al.
propose a quantum algorithm based on interior-point approaches for solving a portfolio optimization problem with affine equality constraints. The portfolio optimization problem (
130
) can be reformulated into their framework by introducing nonnegative slack variables. Their algorithm solves the resulting problem with a quantum complexity of

O\left(n^{1.5}\frac{\zeta\kappa}{\delta^{2}}\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)
,
where
\zeta
,
\kappa
, and
\delta
are problem-dependent parameters related to the conditioning of intermediate solutions. In particular,
\kappa
denotes the maximum condition number of the Newton matrices arising across all iterations of their method. However, since the authors do not provide an explicit characterization of how
\kappa
scales with
n
, the precise dependence of their quantum complexity on the problem dimension remains unclear. This lack of explicit scaling makes it difficult to accurately compare the quantum complexities of their approach with ours. In contrast, the quantum complexity of our Hamiltonian-update-based method does not depend on any unknown parameters. A similar limitation is observed in the interior-point-based quantum algorithm of Dalzell
et al.

[
10
]
for portfolio optimization.

5
Conclusion

In this work, we have shown how binary search combined with Hamiltonian updates can be applied to solve sums-of-squares relaxations of polynomial optimization problems (POPs), both in the unconstrained and the inequality-constrained setting.
By carefully rescaling the problem data and exploiting structural properties of the relaxation, we obtained explicit runtime and accuracy bounds under strong duality and other natural optimality conditions.
Our analysis identifies concrete regimes in which quantum SDP solvers can offer super-quadratic speedups in the problem dimension compared to the best known classical methods, even though they retain a worse dependence on the target precision
\varepsilon
.

A natural direction for future work is to extend these techniques to
sparse
polynomial optimization problems
[
47
,
48
,
30
,
46
]
.
In many practical instances, each constraint and the objective function depend only on a small subset of variables.
Formally, suppose the
n
variables are indexed by
[n]
and there exists a collection of index sets
I_{1},\dots,I_{r}\subseteq[n]
such that:

•

the objective function is a sum of polynomials, each supported on
x(I_{\ell})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\ell}}
,

•

the inequality constraints
(g_{i})_{i\in J_{\ell}}
associated with block
I_{\ell}
depend only on
x(I_{\ell})
and have cardinality
m_{\ell}
.

Such
structured sparsity
allows one to replace the dense moment/SOS relaxation with a block-structured one whose PSD constraints act only on the monomial variables of size
n_{\ell}
for each block.
This dramatically reduces the size of the semidefinite constraints from
\binom{n+k}{n}
to
\binom{n_{\ell}+k}{n_{\ell}}
, and similarly for their degree-
2k
analogues.

If our quantum Hamiltonian-updates approach is applied directly to the block-diagonal SDP arising from such a sparse POP, the runtime bound is expected to improve to

O\!\left(s_{g}\left\{\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\binom{n_{\ell}+2k}{n_{\ell}}\right]^{1/2}+\left[\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}\sum_{i\in J_{\ell}}\binom{n_{\ell}+k-d_{i}}{n_{\ell}}\right]^{1/2}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right\}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right),

where
s_{g}
is the maximal number of nonzero coefficients in any constraint polynomial.
This bound reflects the fact that sparsity reduces both the total number of constraints and the size of each PSD block, which in turn lowers the cost of the quantum subroutines.

Another promising avenue is to investigate alternative semidefinite relaxations that are weaker than the standard Lasserre hierarchy but cheaper to solve, such as diagonally dominant sum-of-squares (DSOS/SDSOS) relaxations
[
1
]
or bounded-degree moment relaxations
[
49
]
.
Combining these with quantum solvers could further enlarge the range of POP instances for which quantum speedups are practically achievable.

Finally, we note that optimization over the simplex
[
11
,
12
]
, which naturally arises in combinatorial optimization and probability distribution optimization, is a particularly compelling application of our framework.
In this case, the problem structure enables sharp bounds on the dual norm, which directly improves the accuracy guarantees of the quantum algorithm.
In summary, this work provides an investigation into the potential of quantum SDP solvers to give speedups for POP relaxations, identifying regimes of superquadratic speedups.

Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

Lemma 25
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
,
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumption
2
holds.
Let
G^{\star}
be an optimal solution to the SOS relaxation (
109
).
Then
\tr(G^{\star})\leq 1
.

Proof.

We write
G^{\star}=\diag(G_{0}^{\star},\dots,G_{m}^{\star})
and assume
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
.
From the SOS relaxation (
109
), we have:

f-\lambda_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}^{\star}v_{k-d_{i}}\,.

(153)

This implies:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu\geq&\int(f-\lambda_{k})d\mu\\[5.0pt]
=&\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tr(G^{\star}_{i}M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}\mu))\\[5.0pt]
=&\tr(G^{\star}D_{k}(g\mu))\geq\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))\tr(G^{\star})\,.\end{array}

(154)

Since
\mu
is supported on
S(g)
, it follows that
\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))\geq 0
. Thus,

\tr(G^{\star})\leq\frac{\int(f-\underline{\lambda})d\mu}{\lambda_{\min}(D_{k}(g\mu))}\leq 1\,.

(155)

The last equality is due to Assumption
2
.
Hence, the result follows.
∎

Lemma 26
.

Let Assumption
3
hold.
Then, for
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
, the matrix
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
defined in (
117
) satisfies
-I\preceq B_{g}^{(\gamma)}\preceq I
, where
I
is the identity matrix of the same size as
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
.

Proof.

With
i=0
, note that
g_{0}=1
satisfies
\|g_{0}\|_{\ell_{1}}=1
.
Fix
i\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
.
By Lemma
17
(
iv
), the matrix
B_{i}^{(\eta)}
satisfies
-I_{i}\preceq B_{i}^{(\eta)}\preceq I_{i}
for all
\eta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2(k-d_{i})}
, where
I_{i}
is the identity matrix of the same size as
B_{i}^{(\eta)}
.
The
i
-th block of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
is given by

B_{\gamma,i}=\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)}\,.

(156)

Using the bounds on
B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)}
, we find

B_{\gamma,i}\preceq\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}|g_{i,\zeta}|I_{i}\preceq\|g_{i}\|_{\ell_{1}}I_{i}\preceq I_{i}

(157)

and similarly,

B_{\gamma,i}\succeq-\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}|g_{i,\zeta}|I_{i}\succeq-\|g_{i}\|_{\ell_{1}}I_{i}\succeq-I_{i}\,.

(158)

Thus, the block-diagonal matrix
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}=\diag(B_{\gamma,0},\dots,B_{\gamma,m})
satisfies

-I=\diag(-I_{0},\dots,-I_{m})\preceq B_{g}^{(\gamma)}\preceq\diag(I_{0},\dots,I_{m})=I\,.

(159)

∎

Lemma 27
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumption
2
holds.
Define
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Let
C
,
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N
be generated by Algorithm
4
.
Then problem (
109
) is equivalent to problem (
50
).
Moreover, this is equivalent to

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(160)

Proof.

By Lemma
25
, problem (
109
) can be written as

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{k}=\sup\limits_{\lambda,G}&\lambda\\
\text{s.t.}&\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,,\,G=\diag(G_{0},\dots,G_{m})\succeq 0\,,\,\tr(G)\leq 1\,,\\
&f-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}\,.\end{array}

(161)

With
G_{i}=((G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta})_{\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}
, the affine constraint
f-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}
expands as:

\begin{array}[]{rl}f-\lambda=&\sum_{i=0}^{m}(\sum_{\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}g_{i,\zeta}x^{\zeta})\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}x^{\alpha+\beta}\\[10.0pt]
=&\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}\sum_{\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}}g_{i,\zeta}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}x^{\alpha+\beta+\zeta}\,.\end{array}

(162)

Equating the coefficients of
x^{\gamma}
, we obtain:

\begin{cases}f_{0}-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta+\zeta=0\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\\
f_{\gamma}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta+\zeta=\gamma\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{cases}

(163)

This simplifies to:

\begin{cases}f_{0}-\lambda=\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i,0}(G_{i})_{0,0}\,,\\
f_{\gamma}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\zeta\end{array}}(G_{i})_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{cases}

(164)

Using
B^{(\eta)}_{i}
as defined in (
116
), the second constraint becomes:

f_{\gamma}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}\tr(B_{i}^{(\gamma-\xi)}G_{i})=\tr(B_{g}^{(\gamma)}G)\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,,

(165)

where
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
is defined as in (
117
).
Similarly,
\lambda=f_{0}-\tr(B_{g}^{(0)}G)
.
By the definition of
\tilde{A}_{i}
and
\tilde{C}
, we have:

\begin{cases}\lambda=f_{0}-\tr(B_{g}^{(0)}G)\Leftrightarrow\lambda=f_{0}-\tr(\tilde{C}G)\,,\\
f_{\gamma_{i}}=\tr(B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})}G)\Leftrightarrow\tilde{b}_{i}=\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{cases}

(166)

Then problem (
161
) becomes

\begin{array}[]{rl}f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\inf\limits_{G}&\tr(\tilde{C}G)\\
\text{s.t.}&G\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\tilde{N}}\,,\,\tr(G)\leq 1\,,\\
&\tr(\tilde{A}_{i}G)=\tilde{b}_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(167)

By the definition of
C
and
A_{i}
, this is equivalent to (
50
).

Similarly, (
160
) follows because removing the constraint
\tr(G)\leq 1
from (
161
) does not affect the optimal value of the problem.
Thus, the result is established.
∎

Lemma 28
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumptions
2
and
3
holds.
Let
C
,
A_{i},b_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
N
be generated by Algorithm
4
.
Set
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Then the following properties hold:

(i)

The maximum number of nonzero entries in any row of
C
and
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, is
s_{g}\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}|\supp(g_{i})|\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}\binom{n+2d_{i}}{n}
.

(ii)

-I_{N}\preceq C\preceq I_{N}
and
-I_{N}\preceq A_{i}\preceq I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.

(iii)

\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.

(iv)

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)=b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}
.

Proof.

(
i
) By the definition of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
in (
117
), for
i=0,\dots,m
, each row of the
i
-th block of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
contains at most
|\supp(g_{i})|\leq|{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}|=\binom{n+2d_{i}}{n}
nonzero entries.
This is because
B_{i}^{(\eta)}
has at most one nonzero entry on each row by Lemma
17
(
i
).
Consequently, each row of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
has at most
s_{g}\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}|\supp(g_{i})|\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}\binom{n+2d_{i}}{n}
nonzero entries.
The same property holds for
C=\diag(B_{g}^{(0)},0)
and
A_{i}=\diag(B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})},0)
.

(
ii
) follows from Lemma
26
and the definitions of
C
and
A_{i}
.

(
iii
) Since
\lambda_{\min}=f_{0}-f(a)
for
a\in S(g)
, and
\lambda_{\max}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}
with
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}\leq f^{\star}\leq f(a)
, we conclude that
\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.

(
iv
) follows from the last statement of Lemma
27
.

∎

Lemma 29
.

Let
C
,
A_{i},b_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
N
be generated by Algorithm
4
.
Assume that strong duality holds for problems (
109
)-(
110
),as well as for problem (
160
) and its dual (
42
).
Let
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, and define
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
such that
y_{0}=1
and
y_{\gamma_{i}}=-\xi_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Then
y
is an optimal solution to the moment relaxation (
110
) if and only if
\xi
is an optimal solution to problem (
42
).

Proof.

By strong duality, it holds that
\tau_{k}=\lambda_{k}
and
\lambda^{\star}=\tau^{\star}
.
From the definition of
b
, the Riesz functional
L_{y}(f)
can be expessed as

L_{y}(f)=y_{0}f_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}f_{\gamma_{j}}=f_{0}-b^{\top}\xi

(168)

Next, we claim the following representation of the localizing matrix:

M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y)=y_{0}B_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}B_{\gamma_{j},i}\,,

(169)

where

B_{\gamma,i}
, the
i
-th block of
B_{g}^{(\gamma)}
, is defined as in (
156
).
To show this, note that:

y_{0}B_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}B_{\gamma_{j},i}=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}B_{\gamma,i}=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)}\,.

(170)

Since
(B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)})_{\alpha,\beta}
equals
1
if
\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\zeta
, and
0
otherwise, the
(\alpha,\beta)
-entry of matrix
y_{0}B_{0,i}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}B_{\gamma_{j},i}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{rl}&\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}(B_{i}^{(\gamma-\zeta)})_{\alpha,\beta}\\
=&\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}y_{\gamma}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\zeta\end{array}}g_{i,\zeta}\\
=&\sum\limits_{\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}g_{i,\zeta}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma=\alpha+\beta+\zeta\end{array}}y_{\gamma}\\
=&\sum\limits_{\zeta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}g_{i,\zeta}y_{\alpha+\beta+\zeta}\,.\end{array}

(171)

This matches the
(\alpha,\beta)
-entry of
M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y)
, confirming (
169
).

From (
169
) and the definition of
\tilde{C},\tilde{A}_{i}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}D_{k}(gy)=&\diag((M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y))_{i=0,\dots,m})\\[5.0pt]
=&y_{0}\diag((B_{0,i})_{i=0,\dots,m})+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}\diag((B_{\gamma_{j},i})_{i=0,\dots,m})\\[5.0pt]
=&y_{0}B_{g}^{(0)}+\sum_{j=1}^{M}y_{\gamma_{j}}B_{g}^{(\gamma_{j})}\\[5.0pt]
=&\tilde{C}-\sum_{j=1}^{M}\xi_{j}\tilde{A}_{j}\,,\end{array}

(172)

where
D_{k}(gy)
is defined as in (
111
).

Thus, we obtain

\diag(D_{k}(gy),0)=C-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}A_{i}=C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi
.
Using this result alongside (
168
), we can conclude the following equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{rl}&y\text{ is an optimal solution for \eqref{eq:sdp.relaxation.cons.pop.dual}}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&D_{k}(gy)\succeq 0\text{ and }L_{y}(f)=\tau_{k}=\lambda_{k}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi\succeq 0\text{ and }b^{\top}\xi=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda^{\star}=\tau^{\star}\\[5.0pt]
\Leftrightarrow&\xi\text{ is an optimal solution for \eqref{eq:dual.no.tr.cons}.}\end{array}

(173)

∎

A.1.1
Proof of Theorem
2

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Since strong duality holds for problems (
109
)-(
110
), it also holds for problem (
160
) and its dual (
42
) by Lemma
27
.

Let
y^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be the truncated moment sequence with respect to the Dirac measure
\delta_{x^{\star}}
, where
y^{\star}_{\alpha}=\int x^{\alpha}d\delta_{x^{\star}}=x^{\star\alpha}
.

We claim that
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
110
).
First, observe that
y^{\star}_{0}=(x^{\star})^{0}=1
and
M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y^{\star})=g_{i}(x^{\star})v_{k-d_{i}}(x^{\star})v_{k-d_{i}}(x^{\star})^{\top}\succeq 0
.
Then
y^{\star}
is a feasible solution for problem (
110
).
Additionally,
L_{y^{\star}}(f)=\int fd\delta_{x^{\star}}=f(x^{\star})=f^{\star}=\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
,where the last equality follows from strong duality. Therefore,
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
110
).
Define
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
.
By Lemma
29
,
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
is optimal for problem (
42
).

The remainder follows similarly to the proof of Theorem
1
using Lemmas
28
and
14
.
∎

A.1.2
Proof of Theorem
3

Proof.

Let
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be a feasible solution for problem (
110
).

We first prove by induction that for
j=1,\dots,k
,

L_{y}(R^{j}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2j})\geq 0\,.

(174)

For
j=1
, we have
L_{y}(R^{j}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2j})=L_{y}(g_{1})\geq 0
since it corresponds to the
(0,0)
-entry of the localizing matrix
M_{k-d_{1}}(g_{1}y)\succeq 0
.
Assume that (
174
) holds for
j=1,\dots,t
with
1\leq t\leq k
.
To prove for
j=t+1
, use the identity:

\begin{array}[]{rl}R^{t+1}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2(t+1)}=R(R^{t}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t})+\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t}(R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2})\,.\end{array}

(175)

Since
\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t}
is a sum-of-squares when
t
is even, it can be expressed as:

\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t}=(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{1}})^{2}=u^{\top}v_{k-d_{1}}v_{k-d_{1}}^{\top}u\,,

(176)

for some real vector
u
.
Applying
L_{y}
to the identity (
175
):

\begin{array}[]{rl}L_{y}(R^{t+1}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2(t+1)})=&L_{y}(R(R^{t}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t}))+L_{y}(\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t}(R-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}))\\[5.0pt]
=&RL_{y}(R^{t}-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2t})+\frac{1}{c}L_{y}(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{1}}v_{k-d_{1}}^{\top}ug_{1})\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\frac{1}{c}L_{y}(u^{\top}(g_{1}v_{k-d_{1}}v_{k-d_{1}}^{\top})u)\\[5.0pt]
=&\frac{1}{c}\tr(uu^{\top}M_{k-d_{1}}(g_{1}y))\geq 0\,,\end{array}

(177)

where the first inequality uses the induction hypothesis, and the second follows from the positive semidefiniteness of
uu^{\top}
and
M_{k-d_{1}}(g_{1}y)
.
Thus, the induction is complete.

Next, we find the upper bound on the trace of the moment matrix
M_{k}(y)
.
From (
174
),

\begin{array}[]{rl}R^{j}=R^{j}y_{0}=L_{y}(R^{j})\geq&L_{y}((x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{n}^{2})^{j})\\[5.0pt]
=&L_{y}(\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}x^{2\gamma})\\[15.0pt]
=&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}y_{2\gamma}\\[15.0pt]
\geq&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}y_{2\gamma}\,,\end{array}

(178)

where the last step follows because
\binom{j}{\gamma}\geq 1
.
Summing over
j=1,\dots,k
, we get

\tr(M_{k}(y))=y_{0}+\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{2\gamma}=1+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}y_{2\gamma}\leq 1+\sum_{j=1}^{k}R^{j}\leq\frac{1}{1-R}\,.

(179)

From this, it follows that

\begin{array}[]{rl}\frac{1}{1-R}\geq&\tr(M_{k}(y))=\|M_{k}(y)\|_{*}\qquad\quad\text{(since $M_{k}(y)\succeq 0$)}\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\|M_{k}(y)\|_{F}=\|\text{vec}(M_{k}(y))\|_{\ell_{2}}\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\|\text{vec}(M_{k}(y))\|_{\ell_{1}}\\[5.0pt]
=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}|y_{2\alpha}|+\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha\neq\beta\end{array}}\sqrt{2}|y_{\alpha+\beta}|\right)\\[15.0pt]
\geq&\frac{1}{\sqrt{L}}\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}|y_{\gamma}|\,,\end{array}

(180)

where
L=\frac{1}{2}\binom{n+k}{n}[\binom{n+k}{n}+1]
is the length of
\text{vec}(M_{k}(y))
.
This implies that with
\tilde{y}=(y_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
,

\|\tilde{y}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}|y_{\gamma}|\leq\frac{\sqrt{L}}{1-R}\,.

(181)

Thus, for
y^{\star}
, the optimal solution to problem (
110
), we have
\|\tilde{y}^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{L}}{1-R}
, where
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
.
Moreover,
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
is optimal for problem (
42
).
The remaining proof follows similarly to the argument in Theorem
2
.
∎

A.1.3
Proof of Theorem
4

Proof.

Let
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be a feasible solution for problem (
110
).

We first prove that
y_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
.

•

If
\gamma=0
, then
y_{\gamma}=y_{0}=1\geq 0
.

•

If
\gamma\neq 0
, there exists
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
such that
\gamma-\alpha\in 2{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
.
Define
\beta=\frac{\gamma-\alpha}{2}
. Then
\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}
, and we have
y_{\gamma}=L_{y}(x^{\gamma})=L_{y}(x^{\alpha+2\beta})=L_{y}(g_{i_{\alpha}}x^{2\beta})
.
This implies
y_{\gamma}
is the
(\beta,\beta)
-entry of the localizing matrix
M_{k-d_{i_{\alpha}}}(g_{i_{\alpha}}y)
.
Since
M_{k-d_{i_{\alpha}}}(g_{i_{\alpha}}y)\succeq 0
, it follows that
y_{\gamma}\geq 0
.

Next, we prove by induction that for
j=1,\dots,2k
,

L_{y}(r^{j}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j})\geq 0\,.

(182)

For
j=1,2
,
L_{y}(r^{j}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j})=L_{y}(g_{i_{j}})\geq 0
as it corresponds to the
(0,0)
-entry of the localizing matrix
M_{k-d_{i_{j}}}(g_{i_{j}}y)\succeq 0
.
Assume that (
182
) holds for
j=1,2,\dots,t
with
3\leq t\leq 2k
.
We prove it for
j=t+1
.
Consider the following two cases:

•

Case 1:
t
is even.
Using the identity

\begin{array}[]{rl}r^{t+1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t+1}=&r(r^{t}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t})\\
&+(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t}(r-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n}))\,,\end{array}

(183)

and noting that
(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t}
is a sum of squares when
t
is even, we write

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t}=(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}})^{2}=u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}^{\top}u\,,

(184)

for some real vector
u
.
Applying
L_{y}
for (
183
), we get

\begin{array}[]{rl}&L_{y}(r^{t+1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t+1})\\[5.0pt]
=&L_{y}(r(r^{t}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t}))\\[5.0pt]
&+L_{y}((x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t}(r-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})))\\[5.0pt]
=&rL_{y}(r^{t}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t})+\frac{1}{c_{1}}L_{y}(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}^{\top}ug_{i_{1}})\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\frac{1}{c_{1}}L_{y}(u^{\top}(g_{i_{1}}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}v_{k-d_{i_{1}}}^{\top})u)=\frac{1}{c_{1}}\tr(uu^{\top}M_{k-d_{i_{1}}}(g_{i_{1}}y))\geq 0\,,\end{array}

(185)

where the first inequality uses the induction hypothesis, and the second follows from the positive semidefiniteness of
uu^{\top}
and
M_{k-d_{i_{1}}}(g_{i_{1}}y)
.

•

Case 2:
t
is odd.
Using the identity

\begin{array}[]{rl}r^{t+1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t+1}=&r^{2}(r^{t-1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1})\\
&+(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1}(r^{2}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{2})\,,\end{array}

(186)

and noting that
(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1}
is a sum-of-squares when
t
is odd, we write

(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1}=(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}})^{2}=u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}^{\top}u\,,

(187)

for some real vector
u
.
Applying
L_{y}
for (
186
), we similarly obtain

\begin{array}[]{rl}&L_{y}(r^{t+1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t+1})\\[5.0pt]
=&L_{y}(r^{2}(r^{t-1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1}))\\[5.0pt]
&+L_{y}((x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1}(r^{2}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{2}))\\[5.0pt]
=&r^{2}L_{y}(r^{t-1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t-1})+\frac{1}{c_{2}}L_{y}(u^{\top}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}^{\top}ug_{i_{2}})\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\frac{1}{c_{2}}L_{y}(u^{\top}(g_{i_{2}}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}v_{k-d_{i_{2}}}^{\top})u)=\frac{1}{c_{2}}\tr(uu^{\top}M_{k-d_{i_{2}}}(g_{i_{2}}y))\geq 0\,.\end{array}

(188)

In both cases,
L_{y}(r^{t+1}-(x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{t+1})\geq 0
, completing the induction.

We now show that
\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}
.
From the inequality (
182
),

\begin{array}[]{rl}r^{j}=r^{j}y_{0}=L_{y}(r^{j})\geq&L_{y}((x_{1}+\dots+x_{n})^{j})\\[5.0pt]
=&L_{y}(\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}x^{\gamma})\\[15.0pt]
=&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}\binom{j}{\gamma}y_{\gamma}\\[10.0pt]
\geq&\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}y_{\gamma}\,,\end{array}

(189)

where the last step follows because
\binom{j}{\gamma}\geq 1
and
y_{\gamma}\geq 0
.
Summing over
j=1,\dots,2k
, we get

\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}=\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\sum_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\\
\scriptstyle|\gamma|=j\end{array}}y_{\gamma}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{2k}r^{j}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}\,.

(190)

With
\tilde{y}=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
, this implies that
\|\tilde{y}\|_{\ell_{1}}=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}

Thus, for
y^{\star}
, the optimal solution to problem (
110
), we have
\|\tilde{y}^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}
, where
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
.
Moreover,
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
is optimal for problem (
42
).
The remainder of the proof follows similarly to the argument in Theorem
2
.
∎

A.1.4
Proof of Lemma
23

Proof.

The most computationally expensive part of Algorithm
4
is the binary search using Hamiltonian updates in Step
2
.
By Lemma
15
, the classical computer requires
O((MsN+N^{\omega})\varepsilon^{-2})
operations, and the quantum computer requires
O(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4})
operations,
where
s
is the maximum number of nonzero entries in a row of the data matrices
C
and
A_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
By Lemma
28
(
i
), we have
s=s_{g}\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}|\supp(g_{i})|
, where the last value is the maximum number of nonzero coefficients of each
g_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,m
.
Here,
M=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
and

N=1+\tilde{N}=1+\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\,.

(191)

Hence the result follows.
∎

A.2
Binary search using Hamiltonian updates for semidefinite programs with affine inequality constraints

Lemma 30
.

Let
b\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
,
C\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and let
\mathcal{A}:\mathbb{S}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
be a linear operator defined by

\mathcal{A}Y=(\tr(A_{1}Y),\dots,\tr(A_{M}Y))\,,

(192)

where
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Consider primal-dual semidefinite programs:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{Y}&\tr(CY)\\
\text{s.t.}&Y\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\mathcal{A}Y\leq b\,,\end{array}

(193)

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tau^{\star}=\sup\limits_{\lambda}&b^{\top}\xi\\
\text{s.t.}&\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\,,\,\lambda\leq 0\,,\,C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi\succeq 0\,,\end{array}

(194)

where
\mathcal{A}^{\top}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to\mathbb{S}^{N}
is the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{A}
, given by

\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}A_{i}\,.

(195)

Assume strong duality holds for problems (
193
)-(
194
):
\lambda^{\star}=\tau^{\star}
, and both problems admit solutions
Y^{\star}
and
\xi^{\star}
, respectively.
Then, for all
X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
, the following hold:

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-\xi^{\star\top}(b-\mathcal{A}X)\,.

(196)

Proof.

Set
Z^{\star}=C-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}
.
Then
Z^{\star}\succeq 0
.
The Lagrangian has the form

L(Y,Z,\xi)=\tr(CY)-\tr(ZY)-\xi^{\top}(\mathcal{A}X-b)\,.

(197)

By the Karush–Kuhn–Tucker (KKT) conditions, we have

0=\frac{\partial L}{\partial Y}(Y^{\star},Z^{\star},\xi^{\star})=C-Z^{\star}-\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}\,.

(198)

\tr(Z^{\star}Y^{\star})=0\,,

(199)

\xi^{\star\top}(\mathcal{A}Y^{\star}-b)=0\,.

(200)

Let
X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}
.
By (
198
),
C=Z^{\star}+\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}
.
Then

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tr(CX)-\lambda^{\star}=&\tr(C(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr((Z^{\star}+\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star})(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr(Z^{\star}X)+\tr(\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}(X-Y^{\star}))\\
=&\tr(Z^{\star}X)+\xi^{\star\top}\mathcal{A}(X-Y^{\star})\\
=&\tr(Z^{\star}X)+\xi^{\star\top}(\mathcal{A}X-b)\\
\geq&\xi^{\star\top}(\mathcal{A}X-b)\,.\end{array}

(201)

The first equality follows from
\tr(CY^{\star})=\lambda^{\star}
, the third equality is due to (
199
), the last equality uses (
200
), and the last inequality is based on the positive semidefiniteness of
Z^{\star}
and
X
.
Hence, the result follows.
∎

Consider the following semidefinite program with trace one:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda^{\star}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(CX)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(X)=1\,,\\
&\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\end{array}

(202)

where
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, are given.
Define the set
S^{(\lambda)}
as

S^{(\lambda)}=\left\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}&\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\\
&\tr(CX)\leq\lambda\,,\,\tr(X)=1\end{array}\right.\right\}\,.

(203)

We propose the following algorithm to numerically solve the SDP (
202
):

Algorithm 6
.

Binary search using Hamiltonian updates

•

Input:
\lambda_{\min}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda_{\max}>\lambda_{\min}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i=1,\dots,M
.

•

Output:
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Set
\underline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda_{\min}
,
\overline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda_{\max}
and
T=\left\lceil\log_{2}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\rceil
.

2.

For
t=0,\dots,T-1
, do

(a)

Set
\lambda_{t}=\frac{\underline{\lambda}_{t}+\overline{\lambda}_{t}}{2}
.

(b)

Run Algorithm
1
(Hamiltonian updates) to test the feasibility of
S^{(\lambda_{t})}
(defined in (
203
)) and obtain
\eta_{t}\in\{0,1\}
and
X_{\varepsilon}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

(c)

Based on
\eta_{t}
:

i.

If
\eta_{t}=0
, set
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{t}
.

ii.

If
\eta_{t}=1
, set
\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\underline{\lambda}_{t}
,
\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\lambda_{t}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=X_{\varepsilon}^{(t)}
.

Define the set:

S^{(\lambda)}_{\varepsilon}=\left\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\left|\begin{array}[]{rl}&\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}+\varepsilon\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,,\\
&\tr(CX)\leq\lambda+\varepsilon\,,\,\tr(X)=1\end{array}\right.\right\}\,.

(204)

Note that
S^{(\lambda)}_{0}=S^{(\lambda)}
.

The following lemma provides a necessary and sufficient condition for
S^{(\lambda)}
to be nonempty:

Lemma 31
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value of the semidefinite program in (
202
).
Assume that the problem (
202
) has an optimal solution.
Define
S^{(\lambda)}
as in (
203
).
Then,

S^{(\lambda)}\neq\emptyset\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\lambda\geq\lambda^{\star}\,.

(205)

The proof of Lemma
31
is similar to that of Lemma
9
.

We present the basic properties of the values returned by binary search in the following two lemmas:

Lemma 32
.

Let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
and
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequences generated by Algorithm
6
.
Then, for
t=0,\dots,T-1
, the following holds:

\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}-\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{2}(\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t})\,.

(206)

The proof of Lemma
32
is similar to Lemma
10
.

Lemma 33
.

Let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
and
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequences generated by Algorithm
6
.
Then, the following statements hold:

1.

For
t=0,\dots,T-1
, the sequence satisfies

\lambda_{\min}\leq\underline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\underline{\lambda}_{t+1}\leq\overline{\lambda}_{t+1}\leq\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\lambda_{\max}\,.

(207)

2.

For
t=0,\dots,T
, we have
\overline{\lambda}_{t}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}=\frac{1}{2^{t}}(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})
. In particular,

\overline{\lambda}_{T}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=\frac{1}{2^{T}}(\lambda_{\max}-\lambda_{\min})\leq\varepsilon\,.

(208)

The proof of Lemma
33
is based on Lemma
32
and similar to Lemma
11
.

Lemma 34
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
, and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
202
), and assume that problem (
202
) has an optimal solution.
Additionally, let
\lambda_{\min}\leq\lambda^{\star}
, and let
(\underline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequence generated by Algorithm
6
.
Then for all
t=0,\dots,T
,

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{t}\,.

(209)

The proof of Lemma
34
is similar to Lemma
12
and based on Lemmas
5
and
31

Lemma 35
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
202
) and assume
\lambda_{\max}\geq\lambda^{\star}
.
Further, assume the following equality holds:

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(210)

Assume strong duality holds for problem (
210
) and its dual (
194
), which admits a solution
\xi^{\star}\geq 0
.
Let
(\overline{\lambda}_{t})_{t=0}^{T}
be the sequence generated in Algorithm
6
.
Then for
t=0,\dots,T
, we have the following bound:

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\varepsilon(1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi^{\star}_{i})\,.

(211)

Proof.

We will prove statement (
211
) by induction, using Lemma
30
.

•

Base case: For
t=0
, we have

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{0}=\lambda^{\star}-\lambda_{\max}\leq 0\leq\varepsilon\left(1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi_{i}^{\star}\right)\,.

(212)

Thus (
211
) holds for
t=0
.

•

Inductive Step:
Assume that (
211
) holds for some
t=r\leq T-1
.
We now show that it holds for
t=r+1
.
Consider Step 2 (c) of Algorithm
6
with
t=r
.

–

Case 1:
\eta_{r}=0
. In this case, we have
\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{r}
. By the induction assumption, it follows that

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}=\overline{\lambda}_{r}\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon\left(1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi^{\star}_{i}\right)\,.

(213)

–

Case 2:
\eta_{r}=1
.
By Lemma
5
, we know that
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
30
, we obtain

\tr(CX_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})\geq\lambda^{\star}-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\lambda^{\star}_{i}[b_{i}-\tr(A_{i}X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})]\geq\lambda^{\star}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{M}\lambda^{\star}_{i}\,.

(214)

Since
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
b_{i}-\tr(A_{i}X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})\geq-\varepsilon
for all
i=1,\dots,M
and the last inequality holds.
Therefore, we have the following estimate:

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}+\varepsilon=\lambda_{r}+\varepsilon\geq\tr(CX_{\varepsilon}^{(r)})\geq\lambda^{\star}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi^{\star}_{i}\,.

(215)

The first inequality is based on
X_{\varepsilon}^{(r)}\in S^{(\lambda_{r})}_{\varepsilon}
.
It implies that

\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}\geq\lambda^{\star}-\varepsilon(1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\lambda^{\star}_{i})\,.

(216)

Combining both cases, we conclude that

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{r+1}\leq\varepsilon\left(1-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi^{\star}_{i}\right)\,.

(217)

Thus, by induction, (
211
) holds for all
t=0,\dots,T
.
∎

Lemma 36

(Accuracy)
.

Let
C,A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
,
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and assume that
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
and
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Assume that problem (
202
) has an optimal solution.
Let
\lambda^{\star}
be the optimal value in (
202
), and let
\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.
Further, assume the following equality holds:

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(218)

Assume strong duality holds for problem (
218
) and its dual (
194
), which admits a solution
\xi^{\star}\geq 0
.
Let
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be the value returned by Algorithm
6
.
Then, the following convergence result holds:

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon(2-\sum_{i=1}^{M}\xi^{\star}_{i})\,.

(219)

The proof of Lemma
36
is similar to that of Lemma
14
and based on Lemmas
34
,
35
, and
33
.

The following lemma presents the complexity of binary search using Hamiltonian updates, as derived from the work of Apeldoorn and Gilyén
[
43
]
:

Lemma 37

(Complexity)
.

The complexity of running Algorithm
6
is as follows:

•

On a classical computer, the algorithm takes

O((MsN+N^{\omega})\varepsilon^{-2})

(220)

operations, where
\omega\approx 2.373
,
N
is the size of the matrix,
M
is the number of affine constraints, and
\varepsilon
is the desired accuracy.

•

On a quantum computer, the algorithm requires

O(s(\sqrt{M}+\sqrt{N}\varepsilon^{-1})\varepsilon^{-4})

(221)

operations, where
s
is the maximum number of nonzero entries in any row of the matrices
C
and
A_{i}
(for
i=1,\dots,M
).

A.3
Inequality constrained polynomial optimization over the simplex

Lemma 38
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
,
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Assume
S(g)\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
.
Suppose Assumption
2
holds.
Let
G^{\star}
be an optimal solution to (
124
).
Then
\tr(G^{\star})\leq 1
.

Proof.

We write
G^{\star}=\diag(G_{0}^{\star},\dots,G_{m}^{\star})
and assume
\underline{\lambda}\leq\lambda_{k}
.
From the SOS relaxation, we have:

\begin{cases}f_{0}-\lambda\geq\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i,0}(G_{i}^{*})_{0,0}\,,\\[5.0pt]
f_{\gamma}\geq\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\xi}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\xi\end{array}}(G_{i}^{*})_{\alpha,\beta}\,,\,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}\,.\end{cases}

(222)

Let
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be the truncated moment sequence with respect to
\mu
.
Since
\mu
is supported on
S(g)\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{n}
, for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
,
y_{\gamma}\geq 0
.
From (
222
),

\begin{array}[]{rl}0\leq&y_{0}(f_{0}-\lambda-\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i,0}(G_{i}^{*})_{0,0})\\[5.0pt]
&+\sum\limits_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}\left(f_{\gamma}-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\gamma-\xi\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}\end{array}}g_{i,\xi}\sum\limits_{\begin{array}[]{cc}\scriptstyle\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k-d_{i}}\\
\scriptstyle\alpha+\beta=\gamma-\xi\end{array}}(G_{i}^{*})_{\alpha,\beta}\right)\\[25.0pt]
=&L_{y}(f-\lambda-\sum_{i=0}^{m}g_{i}v_{k-d_{i}}^{\top}G_{i}^{\star}v_{k-d_{i}})\\[5.0pt]
=&\int(f-\lambda_{k})d\mu-\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tr(G^{\star}_{i}M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}\mu))\end{array}

(223)

This implies that

\int(f-\lambda_{k})d\mu\geq\sum_{i=0}^{m}\tr(G^{\star}_{i}M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}\mu))\,.

(224)

The remaining is similar to the proof of Lemma
25
.
∎

Lemma 39
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumption

holds.
Define
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Let
C
,
A_{i}
, for

,
N
be generated by Algorithm
5
.
Then problem (
124
) is equivalent to problem (
202
).
Moreover, this is equivalent to

\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\tr(CX)\,:\,X\in\mathbb{S}^{N}_{+}\,,\,\tr(A_{i}X)\leq b_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\}\,.

(225)

The proof of Lemma
39
is based on Lemma
38
similar to that of Lemma
27
.

Lemma 40
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumption

holds.
Let
C
,

, for

, and

.

and

, for

.

similar to that of Lemma

.

Lemma 41
.

Let
C
,
A_{i},b_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
N
be generated by Algorithm
5
.
Assume that strong duality holds for problems (
124
)-(
125
),as well as for problem (
225
) and its dual (
194
).
Let
\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
, and define
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
such that
y_{0}=1
and
y_{\gamma_{i}}=-\xi_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
.
Then
y
is an optimal solution to the moment relaxation (
110
) if and only if
\xi
is an optimal solution to problem (

).

The proof of Lemma

is similar to that of Lemma

.

A.3.1
Proof of Theorem
5

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\hat{\lambda}_{k}
.
Since strong duality holds for problems (
124
)-(
125
), it also holds for problem (
160
) and its dual (
194
) by Lemma
39
.

Let
y^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be the truncated moment sequence with respect to the Dirac measure
\delta_{x^{\star}}
, where
y^{\star}_{\alpha}=\int x^{\alpha}d\delta_{x^{\star}}=x^{\star\alpha}
.
Since
x^{\star}\in S(g)\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{+}
,
y^{\star}_{\alpha}\geq 0
.

We claim that
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
125
).
First, observe that
y^{\star}_{0}=(x^{\star})^{0}=1
and
M_{k-d_{i}}(g_{i}y^{\star})=g_{i}(x^{\star})v_{k-d_{i}}(x^{\star})v_{k-d_{i}}(x^{\star})^{\top}\succeq 0
.
Then
y^{\star}
is a feasible solution for problem (
125
).
Additionally,
L_{y^{\star}}(f)=\int fd\delta_{x^{\star}}=f(x^{\star})=f^{\star}=\lambda_{k}=\tau_{k}
,where the last equality follows from strong duality. Therefore,
y^{\star}
is an optimal solution to problem (
125
).
Define
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
.
By Lemma
41
,
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
is optimal for problem (
194
).

The remainder follows similarly to the proof of Theorem
1
by replacing
\lambda_{k}
(resp.
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
) by
\hat{\lambda}_{k}
(resp.
\hat{\lambda}_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
) and using Lemmas
40
and
36
.
∎

A.3.2
Proof of Theorem
6

Proof.

Let
y=(y_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
be a feasible solution for problem (
125
).
Then
y_{\gamma}\geq 0
for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}
.
Similar to the proof of Theorem
4
, we obtain
\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}
.
Thus, for
y^{\star}
, the optimal solution to problem (
125
), we have
\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}^{\star}\leq\frac{r}{1-r}
, where
\tilde{y}^{\star}=(y^{\star}_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
41
,
-\tilde{y}^{\star}
is optimal for problem (
194
).
By Lemmas
40
and
36
,
0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon(2+\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}\backslash\{0\}}y_{\gamma}^{\star})\leq\varepsilon(2+\frac{r}{1-r})
.
Recall that
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
. Substituting, we get:

\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}=\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-f^{\star}
.
Thus, the result follows.
∎

Appendix B
Additional quantum complexities for solving SDP relaxations of polynomial optimization

In this appendix, we present some quantum complexities for solving the semidefinite relaxations (
16
)-(
17
) of the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) under Slater’s condition. Our approach in this appendix also utilizes binary search and Hamiltonian updates.

This method applies to the following cases, under the assumption that strong duality holds for the primal-dual semidefinite relaxations (
16
)-(
17
).

For the first two cases, we use the same rescaling coefficient on the objective polynomial
f
as described in the main body of the paper to derive an upper bound on the trace of the matrix variable in the semidefinite program corresponding to the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
).
We assume that Slater’s condition holds for the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
), meaning there exists a strictly feasible solution
\diag(G_{0}^{,}\dots,G_{m})\succ 0
for the sum-of-squares relaxation (
16
) with conditional number
\kappa
.

•

Case 1: Unconstrained polynomial optimization
(see Appendix
B.1.1
).

Consider the unconstrained case of problem (
11
), where
m=l=0
.

Algorithm
3
provides an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of the minimum of

\varepsilon\kappa\binom{n+k}{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\varepsilon\kappa\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}

(226)

(see Theorem
8
).

•

Case 2: Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization problem
(see Appendix
B.1.2
).

Now consider the inequality-constrained case of problem (
11
), where
l=0
.

Our quantum Algorithm
7
achieves a runtime of

\footnotesize O\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}^{2}+\left[\binom{n+k}{n}\binom{n+2k}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}\right)^{\omega}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

(227)

to compute an approximate value for
\lambda_{k}
with an accuracy of

\varepsilon\kappa\binom{n+2k}{n}^{1/2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}|g_{i,0}|\,,

(228)

where
g_{i,0}
is the constant coefficient of
g_{i}
(see Theorem
9
).
Note that the term without
\varepsilon
in the complexity (
227
) represents the time required to prepare the data using the Gram-Schmidt process.

In the following two cases, we formulate the moment relaxation (
17
) as a standard semidefinite program with a constant or bounded trace property, specifically when the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) includes a sphere or ball constraint.
We also assume that Slater’s condition holds for the moment relaxation (
17
), that is, there exists a feasible solution
y
such that

\diag(M_{k}(y),M_{k-d_{1}}(g_{1}y),\dots,M_{k-d_{m}}(g_{m}y))\succ 0

(229)

with a condition number denoted by
\kappa
.
Note that in these last two cases, the coefficients of the objective polynomial
f
and the constraint polynomials
g_{i}
are not rescaled, unlike in the first two cases.

•

Case 3: Equality-constrained polynomial optimization over the unit sphere
(see Appendix
B.2.1
).

Consider the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) with
m=0
,
l\geq 1
, and
h_{1}=1-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
.
In this scenario, our quantum Algorithm
8
for solving the moment relaxation (
17
) has a complexity of

\footnotesize O\left(\binom{n+k}{n}^{1.5}\left\{(l-1)\binom{n+2k}{n}\binom{n+k}{n}^{2.5}+\binom{n+k}{n}^{4.5}+\left[\binom{n+k}{n}^{0.5}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right\}\right)\,.

(230)

This algorithm computes an approximate value for
\tau_{k}
with accuracy

\varepsilon 2^{k}\|f\|_{\ell_{1}}\kappa\binom{n+k}{n}\max\limits_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{k}}\binom{k}{(k-|\alpha|,\alpha)}\,,

(231)

where
\binom{k}{(\alpha_{0},\alpha)}
, with
\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}=k
, denotes the multinomial coefficient (see Theorem
10
).

•

Case 4: (In)equality-constrained polynomial optimization over the unit ball
(see Appendix
B.2.2
).

Consider the polynomial optimization problem (
11
) with
m\geq 1
, and
g_{1}=1-\|x\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2}
.
In this case, our quantum Algorithm
9
for solving the moment relaxation (
17
) has a complexity of

\tiny O\left(\binom{n+k}{n}^{1.5}\left\{m(l-1)\binom{n+k}{n}^{0.5}\binom{n+2k}{n}+m^{3}\binom{n+k}{n}^{4.5}+m^{0.5}\left[\binom{n+k}{n}^{0.5}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right]\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{4}}\right\}\right)\,.

(232)

This algorithm computes an approximate value for
\tau_{k}
with accuracy

\varepsilon 2^{k}\|f\|_{\ell_{1}}\kappa r_{g,n,k}m^{1/2}\binom{n+k}{n}\,,

(233)

where
r_{g,n,k}
is a positive constant depending on
g_{i},n,k
(see Theorem
11
).

Here,
\|f\|_{\ell_{1}}
represents the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the vector of coefficients of polynomial
f
.
Note that the terms without
\varepsilon
in the complexities (
230
) and (
232
) correspond to the time required for data preparation using Gaussian elimination and the Gram-Schmidt process.

In Table
3
we summarize the results for the cases considered above.

Limitations.

The above results are obtained under the Slater condition, and in particular, the error bounds on the optimal values of the SDP relaxations depend on the condition number
\kappa
of the Slater point in the feasible region of these relaxations.
However, finding such a Slater point is generally difficult; in fact, identifying one is equivalent to solving a semidefinite feasibility problem of the same size as the SDP under consideration. Moreover, the condition number
\kappa
of the Slater point may itself depend on the problem size. These assumptions therefore make the results somewhat less convincing than those presented in the main part of the paper.

sum-of-squares and moment relaxations (

)-(

inequality constraints and

equality constraints. Here
g
(resp.
h
) is the set of inequality (resp. equality) constraints in (

);
\kappa
is the condition number of some strictly feasible solution of relaxations;
n_{k}
stands for
\binom{n+k}{n}
;
n_{m,k}
stands for
\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}
; and
n_{m,k}^{(2)}
stands for
\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}^{2}
.

B.1
Other quantum complexities for solving sum-of-squares relaxations

Lemma 42
.

Let all assumptions and notations in Lemma
8
hold.
Define the matrix
A
associated with
\mathcal{A}
as

A=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(A_{1})^{\top}\\
\dots\\
\text{vec}(A_{M})^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(234)

If
\lambda^{\star}\geq 0
,
A
has full rank
M
and Slater’s condition holds for the primal problem (
41
) (i.e., there exists
U\succ 0
such that
\mathcal{A}U=b
), then:

(i)

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-(1+\sqrt{2}N\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
.

(ii)

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}\sqrt{M}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}
.

Proof.

(i) Define
c=\text{vec}(C)
and
z^{\star}=\text{vec}(Z^{\star})
.
Rewriting the first KKT condition (
46
) in vector form gives:

c-z^{\star}-A^{\top}\xi^{\star}=0
,
which implies:

\xi^{\star}=(AA^{\top})^{-1}A(c-z^{\star})\,.

(235)

From this, we obtain:

\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\|c-z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}(\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}+\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\,.

(236)

Combining this result with the bound (
49
), it follows:

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-(\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}+\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}})\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.

(237)

Assume
\lambda^{\star}\geq 0
,
A
has full rank
M
, and that Slater’s condition holds for problem (
41
), i.e., there exists
U\succ 0
such that
\mathcal{A}U=b
.
Multiplying the first KKT condition (
46
) by
U-Y^{\star}
and taking the trace, we have:

\tr(C(U-Y^{\star}))-\tr(Z^{\star}(U-Y^{\star}))-\tr(\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}(U-Y^{\star}))=0

(238)

Since
\tr(Z^{\star}Y^{\star})=0
and
\tr(\mathcal{A}^{\top}\xi^{\star}(U-Y^{\star}))=\xi^{\star\top}\mathcal{A}(U-Y^{\star})=\xi^{\star\top}(b-b)=0
, we deduce:

\tr(CU)-\lambda^{\star}-\tr(Z^{\star}U)=0

(239)

Since
\lambda^{\star}>0
, it follows that:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\lambda_{\min}(U)\tr(Z^{\star})\leq&\tr(Z^{\star}U)=\tr(CU)-\lambda^{\star}\\
\leq&c^{\top}u\leq\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\|u\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,,\end{array}

(240)

where
u=\text{vec}(U)
.
The first inequality is derived from the positive semidefiniteness of
Z^{\star}
.
Let
U=(U_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}
.
Since
U\succeq 0
, we have
0\leq U_{ii}\leq\lambda_{\max}(U)
.
This follows from
0\leq e_{i}^{\top}Ue_{i}\leq\lambda_{\max}(U)e_{i}^{\top}Ie_{i}
, where
e_{i}=(0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)
, since
0\preceq U\preceq\lambda_{\max}(U)I
.
Since
U\succeq 0
, it holds that:
\begin{bmatrix}U_{ii}&U_{ij}\\
U_{ij}&U_{jj}\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0
.
This implies
U_{ij}^{2}\leq U_{ii}U_{jj}\leq\lambda_{\max}(U)^{2}
, and thus
|U_{ij}|\leq\lambda_{\max}(U)
.
Consequently:

\|u\|_{\ell_{\infty}}=\max_{i\leq j}|U_{ij}|\sqrt{2-\delta_{ij}}\leq\lambda_{\max}(U)\sqrt{2}\,.

(241)

From (
240
), we deduce:

\lambda_{\min}(U)\tr(Z^{\star})\leq\sqrt{2}\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\lambda_{\max}(U)\,.

(242)

This implies:

\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}=\|Z^{\star}\|_{F}\leq\|Z^{\star}\|_{*}=\tr(Z^{\star})\leq\sqrt{2}\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\kappa(U)\,.

(243)

With
L=\frac{N(N+1)}{2}\leq N^{2}
, the length of
z^{\star}
, it follows that:

\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{1}}\leq\sqrt{L}\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}\leq N\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}\leq\sqrt{2}N\kappa(U)\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\,.

(244)

Using (
237
), we obtain

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-(1+\sqrt{2}N\kappa(U))\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.

(245)

(ii) From (
48
), we have:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\tr(CX)\geq&\lambda^{\star}-\|\xi^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{2}}\\[5.0pt]
=&\lambda^{\star}-\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A(c-z^{\star})\|_{\ell_{2}}\sqrt{M}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\\[5.0pt]
\geq&\lambda^{\star}-\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}}(\|c\|_{\ell_{2}}+\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}})\sqrt{M}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.\end{array}

(246)

The second inequality follows from (
235
).
Using (
243
), it follows that:

\|c\|_{\ell_{2}}+\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}\leq\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}+\|z^{\star}\|_{\ell_{2}}\leq(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa(U))\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\,.

(247)

To compute
\|A^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{-1}\|_{\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}}
, note:

\begin{array}[]{rl}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{2}\to\ell_{2}}=&\sigma_{\max}((AA^{\top})^{-1}A)=\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}((AA^{\top})^{-1}AA^{\top}(AA^{\top})^{-1})}\\[5.0pt]
=&\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}((AA^{\top})^{-1})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(AA^{\top})}}=\frac{1}{\sigma_{\min}(A)}\,.\end{array}

(248)

Combining this with (
246
) and (
247
), we conclude:

\tr(CX)\geq\lambda^{\star}-\sqrt{M}(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa(U))\|c\|_{\ell_{1}}\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}\|\mathcal{A}X-b\|_{\ell_{\infty}}\,.

(249)

∎

Remark 9
.

Lemma
42
provides lower bounds on the objective function of an SDP, which depend on the condition number of a Slater point
U
, assumed to exist. In certain cases, such a point
U
can be explicitly constructed. However, the resulting bounds tend to involve significantly worse dimensional factors. We will derive these bounds explicitly for the problems of interest later.

Lemma 43
.

Let all assumptions and notations in Lemma
13
hold.
If
\lambda^{\star}\geq 0
,
A
has full rank
M
, and Slater’s condition holds for the primal problem (
61
) (i.e., there exists
U\succ 0
such that
\mathcal{A}U=b
), then:

(i)

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\varepsilon\left[1+(1+\sqrt{2}N\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\right]
.

(ii)

\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{t}\leq\varepsilon\left[1+(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}\sqrt{M}\right]
.

The proof of Lemma
43
is similar to the proof of Lemma
13
and based on Lemma
42
.

Lemma 44

(Accuracy)
.

Let all assumptions and notations in Lemma
14
hold.
If
\lambda^{\star}\geq 0
,
A
has full rank
M
and Slater’s condition holds for the primal problem (
61
) (i.e., there exists
U\succ 0
such that
\mathcal{A}U=b
), then:

(i)

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon\left[2+(1+\sqrt{2}N\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\right]
.

(ii)

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon\left[2+(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa(U))\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\sigma_{\min}(A)^{-1}\sqrt{M}\right]
.

The proof of Lemma
44
is similar to the proof of Lemma
14
and based on Lemma
43
.

B.1.1
Unconstrained polynomial optimization

Lemma 45
.

Let all assumptions and notations in Lemma
20
hold.
Define

A=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(A_{1})^{\top}\\
\dots\\
\text{vec}(A_{M})^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(250)

The following properties hold:

(i)

A
has rank
M
.

(ii)

\sigma_{\min}(A)\geq 1
.

(iii)

\|(A{A}^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\leq 1
.

(iv)

\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}=1
.

Proof.

(
i
) To show
A_{1},\dots,A_{M}
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{S}^{N}
, assume
w\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
such that
0=w_{1}A_{1}+\dots+w_{M}A_{M}
.
Substituting
A_{i}=\diag(B^{(\gamma_{i})},0)
, we have
0=w_{1}B^{(\gamma_{1})}+\dots+w_{M}B^{(\gamma_{M})}
.
By Lemma
17
(
vi
), this implies
w_{1}=\dots=w_{M}=0
.
Hence,
A
has rank
M
.

(
ii
) Writing
A
as

A=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{1})},0))^{\top}\\
\dots\\
\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{M})},0))^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\,,

(251)

and its transpose as

A^{\top}=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{1})},0))&\dots&\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{M})},0))\end{bmatrix}\,.

(252)

For
i=1,\dots,M
, the inner product

a_{i}=\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{j}},0))^{T}\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{j}},0))=\tr(B^{(\gamma_{j})2})\,.

(253)

satisfies
a_{i}\geq 1
by Lemma
17
(
v
). For
i\neq j
,

\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{i}},0))^{T}\text{vec}(\diag(B^{(\gamma_{j}},0))=\tr(B^{(\gamma_{i})}B^{(\gamma_{j})})=0\,.

(254)

Thus,
AA^{\top}=\diag(a_{1},\dots,a_{M})
, and
\lambda_{\min}(AA^{\top})\geq\min\{a_{1},\dots,a_{M}\}\geq 1
.
It follows that
\sigma_{\min}(A)=\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(AA^{\top})}\geq 1
.

(
iii
) From the diagonal structure of
A{A}^{\top}
, we have

(A{A}^{\top})^{-1}=\diag(a_{1}^{-1},\dots,a_{M}^{-1})

(255)

Thus,

(A{A}^{\top})^{-1}A=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}^{-1}\text{vec}(B^{(\gamma_{1})})^{\top}\\
a_{2}^{-1}\text{vec}(B^{(\gamma_{2})})^{\top}\\
\dots\\
a_{M}^{-1}\text{vec}(B^{(\gamma_{M})})^{\top}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{1})}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}&a_{1}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{1})}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}&\dots&a_{1}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{1})}_{\alpha_{L},\beta_{L}}\\
a_{2}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{2})}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}&a_{2}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{2})}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}&\dots&a_{2}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{2})}_{\alpha_{L},\beta_{L}}\\
.&.&\dots&.\\
a_{M}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{M})}_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}&a_{M}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{M})}_{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}}&\dots&a_{M}^{-1}B^{(\gamma_{M})}_{\alpha_{L},\beta_{L}}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(256)

By Lemma
17
(
ii
), each column
i
of this matrix has a unique nonzero entry,
a_{j}^{-1}B_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}^{(\gamma_{j})}
, for some
j\in\{1,\dots,M\}
, and this entry is in the range
(0,1]
.
Therefore, we have
\|{A}^{\top}(A{A}^{\top})^{-1}\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\leq 1
.

(
iv
) Since
C=\diag(B^{(0)},0)=\diag(1,0,\dots,0)
,
\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}=1
.

∎

Theorem 8
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2k}
, and suppose that Assumption
1
holds.
Assume strong duality holds for primal-dual semidefinite relaxations (
76
)-(
77
).
Let
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}
be the value returned by Algorithm
3
.
If Slater’s condition holds for the primal relaxation (
76
) (i.e., it has a feasible solution
G_{n,k}\succ 0
with condition number
\kappa
), then:

(i)

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}\leq\varepsilon\left[3+\sqrt{2}\kappa\left(\binom{n+k}{n}+1\right)\right]
.

(ii)

0\leq\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}\leq\varepsilon\left[2+(1+\sqrt{2}\kappa)\sqrt{\binom{n+2k}{n}-1}\ \right]
.

Proof.

Let
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}
.
Since strong duality holds for problems (
76
)-(
77
), it holds for problem (
96
) and its dual (
42
) by Lemma
19
.

(i) Assume Slater’s condition holds for the primal relaxation (
76
) (i.e., it has a feasible solution
G_{n,k}\succ 0
with condition number
\kappa
).
Define
U_{n,k}=\diag(G_{n,k},\lambda_{\min}(G_{n,k}))
. Since
\lambda_{\min}(G_{n,k})>0
, it follows that
U_{n,k}\succ 0
and also has the same condition number
\kappa
.
Since
\tr(A_{i}U_{n,k})=\tr(B^{(\gamma_{i})}G_{n,k})=f_{\gamma_{i}}=b_{i}
,
U_{n,k}
is a feasible solution for problem (
96
).
This implies that Slater’s condition holds for problem (
96
).
By Lemma
45
(
i
),
A
has full rank
M
.
Since
\lambda_{k}\leq f^{\star}\leq f(0)=f_{0}
, it follows that
\lambda^{\star}=f_{0}-\lambda_{k}\geq 0
.
By Lemma
20
, (
i
) and (
ii
),
I_{N}\succeq C\succeq-I_{N}
,
I_{N}\succeq A_{i}\succeq-I_{N}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
, and
\lambda^{\star}\in[\lambda_{\min},\lambda_{\max}]
.
By Lemma
19
, (
96
) holds.
By Lemma
44
(i), we have

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\overline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon\left[2+(1+\sqrt{2}N\kappa)\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}\|(AA^{\top})^{-1}A\|_{\ell_{1}\to\ell_{1}}\right]\,.

(257)

Using properties of
C
and
A
from Lemma
45
(
iii
) and (
iv
), this simplifies to:
0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon\left(3+\sqrt{2}N\kappa\right)
.
Since
N=\binom{n+k}{n}+1
, we have

0\leq\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}\leq\varepsilon\left[3+\sqrt{2}\left(\binom{n+k}{n}+1\right)\kappa\right]\,.

(258)

Recall that
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}
. Substituting,

\lambda^{\star}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}=\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}-\lambda_{k}
, which proves (i).

The proof of (ii) follows similarly.
∎

B.1.2
Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

Algorithm 7
.

Inequality-constrained polynomial optimization

•

Input:
\varepsilon>0
,
\underline{\lambda}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
a\in S(g)
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(f_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}}
of
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
,

1.1cm
coefficients
(g_{i,\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2d_{i}}}
of
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]
, for
i=0,\dots,m
.

•

Output:
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
G_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

1.

Convert relaxation to standard semidefinite program

(a)

Let
{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}=\{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{M}\}
with
\gamma_{0}=0
, and set
M=|{\mathbb{N}}^{n}_{2k}|-1=\binom{n+2k}{n}-1
.

(b)

Define
\tilde{b}_{i}=f_{\gamma_{i}}
,
\tilde{A}_{i}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})}
, for
i=0,\dots,M
,
\tilde{C}=\tilde{A}_{0}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{0})}=B_{g}^{(0)}
,
\tilde{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+k-d_{i}}{n}
.

(c)

Define
\check{C}=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
,
\check{A}_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
,
\check{b}_{i}=\tilde{b}_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N=\tilde{N}+1
.

(d)

Apply Algorithm
11
(Gram-Schmidt process) to obtain
A_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
and
b_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
i=1,\dots,M
.

(e)

Set
c_{g}=\sqrt{1+g_{1,0}^{2}+\dots+g_{m,0}^{2}}
, where
g_{i,0}
is the constant coefficient of
g_{i}
.

(f)

Set
C=\frac{\check{C}}{c_{g}}
,
\lambda_{\min}=\frac{f_{0}-f(a)}{c_{g}}
and
\lambda_{\max}=\frac{f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}}{c_{g}}
.

2.

Run Algorithm
2
(Binary search using Hamiltonian updates)
to get
\underline{\lambda}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
X_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,N}\in\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

3.

Extract approximate results: Set
\lambda_{k}^{(\varepsilon)}=f_{0}-\underline{\lambda}_{T}c_{g}
and
G_{\varepsilon}=((X_{\varepsilon})_{ij})_{i,j=1,\dots,\tilde{N}}
.

Lemma 46
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose Assumption
2
holds.
Let
\check{C}
,
\check{A}_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N
be generated by Algorithm
7
.
Then problem (
109
) is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{rl}f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(\check{C}X)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{N}\,,\,\tr(X)=1\,,\\
&\tr(\check{A}_{i}X)=\check{b}_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(259)

Moreover, this is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{rl}f_{0}-\lambda_{k}=\inf\limits_{X}&\tr(\check{C}X)\\
\text{s.t.}&X\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{N}\,,\\
&\tr(\check{A}_{i}X)=\check{b}_{i}\,,\,i=1,\dots,M\,.\end{array}

(260)

The proof of Lemma
46
follows the same reasoning as the proof of Lemma
27
.

Lemma 47
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Let
\check{C}
,
\check{A}_{i}
,for
i=1,\dots,M
,
N
, and
c_{g}
be generated by Algorithm
7
.
Then the following properties hold:

(i)

\check{A}_{1},\dots,\check{A}_{M}
are linearly independent in
\mathbb{S}^{N}
.

(ii)

\|\check{C}\|_{F}=c_{g}
.

(iii)

The maximum number of nonzero entries in any row of
\check{C}
is
1
.

Proof.

(
i
)
Suppose
u\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
satisfies
u_{1}\check{A}_{1}+\dots+u_{M}\check{A}_{M}=0
.
Since
\check{A}_{i}=\diag(\tilde{A}_{i},0)
, this implies
u_{1}\tilde{A}_{1}+\dots+u_{M}\tilde{A}_{M}=0
.
From the construction of
\tilde{A}_{i}=B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})}
, this reduces to
u_{1}B_{g}^{(\gamma_{1})}+\dots+u_{M}B_{g}^{(\gamma_{M})}=0
.
Since
g_{0}=1
, the first block of
B_{g}^{(\gamma_{j})}
is
B_{0}^{(\gamma_{i})}=B^{(\gamma_{i})}
, as defined in (
84
).
Thus,
u_{1}B^{(\gamma_{1})}+\dots+u_{M}B^{(\gamma_{M})}=0
.
By Lemma
17
(
vi
),
u_{1}=\dots=u_{M}=0
.
This establishes linear independence.

(
ii
) Recall that
\check{C}=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
, where

\tilde{C}=B_{g}^{(0)}=\diag(g_{0,0},0,\dots,0,g_{1,0},0,\dots,0,g_{m,0},0,\dots,0)\,,

(261)

with
g_{i,0}
denoting the constant coefficient of
g_{i}
.
Since
g_{0,0}=1
, it follows that
\|\check{C}\|_{F}=c_{g}
.

(
iii
) Additionally, the diagonal structure of
\tilde{C}
implies that at most one nonzero entry exists per row of
\check{C}=\diag(\tilde{C},0)
.
∎

Remark 10
.

By Lemma
47
(
i
), the matrix

\check{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(\check{A}_{1})\\
\dots\\
\text{vec}(\check{A}_{M})\end{bmatrix}

(262)

has rank
M
.
However, unlike the unconstrained case, estimating a positive lower bound for the smallest singular value of
\check{A}
is challenging. This bound is crucial for analyzing the accuracy of the algorithm’s output.
The difficulty arises because the entries of
\check{A}_{i}
depend on the coefficients of
g_{i}
, which can take arbitrary values.

To address this, we apply the Gram-Schmidt process to transform
\check{A}_{1},\dots,\check{A}_{M}
into an orthogonal basis
A_{1},\dots,A_{M}
(see Step 1 (d) of Algorithm
7
).

Remark 11
.

By Lemma
28
(
i
), each row of
\check{A}_{i}=\diag(B_{g}^{(\gamma_{i})},0)
has at most
\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}|\supp(g_{i})|\leq\max\limits_{i=0,\dots,m}\binom{n+2d_{i}}{n}
nonzero entries.

However, this sparsity is generally lost for
A_{j}
, as the Gram-Schmidt process transforms
\check{A}_{i}
into a new orthogonal basis
A_{j}
, making each
A_{j}
a linear combination of the
\check{A}_{i}
matrices.

Lemma 48
.

Let
f\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d}
and
g_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}[x]_{2d_{i}}
, for
i=0,\dots,m
, with
g_{0}=1
.
Suppose that Assumption
2
holds.
Let
C
,
A_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,M
,
N
,
c_{g}
,
\lambda_{\min}
, and
\lambda_{\max}
be generated by Algorithm
7
.
Define

A=\begin{bmatrix}\text{vec}(A_{1})\\
\dots\\
\text{vec}(A_{M})\end{bmatrix}\,.

(263)

Set
\lambda^{\star}=\frac{f_{0}-\lambda_{k}}{c_{g}}
.
Then the following hold:

(i)

-I_{N}\preceq C\preceq I_{N}
and
\|\text{vec}(C)\|_{\ell_{1}}=\frac{|g_{0,0}|+\dots+|g_{m,0}|}{c_{g}}
.

(ii)

The number of nonzero entries on each row of
A_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,M
, is no larger than
